Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Client Rights (Management of Care), Psychosocial (Mental Health, Crisis Intervention)

WHAT: Restraints are any device, method, or technique that restricts a patient's movement, physical activity, or normal access to their body. This includes physical restraints (wrist restraints, vests, side rails used as restraints), chemical restraints (medications given to control behavior), and seclusion.

WHY IT MATTERS: Restraints carry serious risks — strangulation, nerve damage, skin breakdown, aspiration, and psychological trauma. They are always a last resort. The NCLEX tests restraint use extensively because improper use is both dangerous and a violation of patient rights.

MECHANISM:
- Least restrictive first: Always use the least restrictive intervention before escalating. Try verbal de-escalation, reorientation, addressing underlying needs (pain, toileting, fear), and environmental modifications first.
- Provider order required: A provider must order restraints. In an emergency, the nurse may apply restraints and obtain the order promptly after — but the order cannot be indefinite.
- Time-limited orders: Orders must be renewed periodically:
- Adults (18+): every 4 hours for behavioral health, every 24 hours for medical-surgical
- Adolescents (9–17): every 2 hours for behavioral health
- Children (under 9): every 1 hour for behavioral health
- Standing orders and PRN orders for restraints are never acceptable.
- Continuous monitoring required: Assess circulation, sensation, and movement (CSM) at least every 2 hours. Offer food, fluids, toileting, and repositioning. Release restraints at regular intervals.

RECOGNIZE:
- Indications for restraints: imminent danger to self or others, disruption of life-sustaining equipment despite other interventions
- Contraindications: using restraints for staff convenience, as punishment, or for a confused patient who could be managed with alternative interventions
- Complications: impaired circulation (check distal pulses, sensation, skin color), skin breakdown, aspiration, psychological distress, nerve damage
- Alternatives to restraints: bed alarms, sitters, reorientation, environmental changes (move patient closer to nurses' station), addressing root cause (pain, confusion, medication effect)

ACT:
1. Exhaust all alternatives before applying restraints
2. Obtain (or ensure) a provider order — never PRN, never standing
3. Use the least restrictive device that addresses the safety concern
4. Ensure proper fit: you should be able to slide two fingers between the restraint and the patient's skin
5. Assess at least every 2 hours: CSM checks, release for ROM, toileting, hydration, nutrition, repositioning
6. Document: reason for restraint, type used, time applied, assessments, patient response, alternatives attempted
7. Reassess the continued need regularly — discontinue as soon as safely possible

MEDS:
- Chemical restraints (e.g., haloperidol, lorazepam): Used for acute behavioral emergencies. Subject to the same time-limited order requirements as physical restraints. Why? These medications sedate and restrict the patient's ability to act autonomously, making them restraints by definition.

CONNECT:
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Client Rights — restraints restrict fundamental rights and must be justified
→ See Section 4 (Psychosocial): Crisis Intervention — de-escalation should precede restraint use
→ See Section 4 (Psychosocial): Mental Health Disorders — behavioral situations where restraints may be needed

TEST TIP: Restraint questions? Two default correct answers: "try something less restrictive first" and "check circulation every 2 hours." Before applying restraints, pick de-escalation. While restraints are on, pick assessment and monitoring. Know the order-renewal time limits by age group — that's a frequent flyer on the exam. And remember: all four side rails up = restraint. Two upper side rails up = not a restraint.

PRACTICE:
A patient with dementia is attempting to pull out their IV line despite multiple redirection attempts. The nurse applies bilateral soft wrist restraints. Which action is the nurse's priority?
A. Document the reason for restraint application
B. Obtain a provider order for the restraints
C. Assess circulation, sensation, and movement in both hands
D. Notify the patient's family

Answer: B. Although the nurse acted appropriately in an emergency, a provider order must be obtained as soon as possible. Assessment (Option C) is critical and ongoing but the order is the immediate next step to provide the legal basis for the restraint. Documentation (A) and family notification (D) are important but come after securing the order. Why B works: Restraints cannot legally continue without a provider order — obtaining one is the immediate priority after emergency application.

Fall Prevention

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Restraints, Medication Safety, all clinical sections

WHAT: Fall prevention encompasses the systematic assessment, identification, and mitigation of fall risk factors in the healthcare setting. Falls are the most commonly reported adverse event in hospitals.

WHY IT MATTERS: A fall can turn a three-day admission into a three-month recovery — or worse. Fractures, head injuries, death. Most of them are preventable with proper assessment and intervention, which is exactly why this is a National Patient Safety Goal and why the NCLEX won't let you forget it.

MECHANISM:
Risk factors for falls:
- Intrinsic: Advanced age, history of previous falls, altered mental status/confusion, impaired mobility or gait, orthostatic hypotension, incontinence/urgency, visual impairment, neuropathy
- Extrinsic: Unfamiliar environment, wet floors, clutter, poor lighting, improper footwear, equipment (IV poles, oxygen tubing), sedating medications
- Medications that increase fall risk: Sedatives/hypnotics, opioids, antihypertensives (orthostatic hypotension), diuretics, antihistamines, psychotropics, polypharmacy (4+ medications)

RECOGNIZE:
- High fall risk indicators: previous fall during admission, confusion/disorientation, unsteady gait, getting out of bed unassisted despite instructions, use of multiple high-risk medications, recent anesthesia or sedation
- Fall risk assessment tools: Morse Fall Scale, Hendrich II Fall Risk Model — use at admission and with any change in condition

ACT:
1. Assess fall risk on admission and with every status change
2. Orient patients to the environment: bed controls, call light location, bathroom
3. Keep bed in the lowest position with wheels locked
4. Ensure call light is within reach at all times
5. Non-skid footwear for ambulatory patients
6. Adequate lighting, clear pathways
7. Assist with toileting on a schedule for patients with urgency/incontinence
8. Use bed alarms for patients who are confused or impulsive
9. Review medications for fall risk contributors — consult pharmacy if needed
10. If a fall occurs: assess for injury first, then notify the provider, complete an incident report, implement additional fall prevention measures

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2: Restraints — restraints are NOT a fall prevention strategy (they can increase injury risk)
→ See Section 2: Error Reporting — fall events require incident reporting
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Orthostatic Hypotension, Neurological Assessment

TEST TIP: The NCLEX loves to ask about the nurse's first action after a patient falls: always assess the patient for injury before anything else — before moving them, before calling the provider, before documenting. Also know that bed alarms are preferable to restraints for fall prevention in confused patients. If a question asks which patient is at highest risk for falls, look for the combination of multiple risk factors — especially altered mental status + mobility impairment + high-risk medications.

PRACTICE:
A nurse enters a room and finds a patient on the floor. The patient states, "I slipped getting to the bathroom." What is the nurse's first action?
A. Call for help and notify the provider immediately
B. Assess the patient for injuries before moving them
C. Help the patient back to bed and apply bilateral side rails
D. Complete an incident report and document the fall

Answer: B. Always assess the patient before taking any other action. Moving a patient who may have a fracture or spinal injury could worsen the injury. After assessment, notify the provider, implement interventions, and complete documentation. Why B works: Assessment always comes first — you need to know whether the patient is injured before deciding on next steps.

Fire Safety & Emergency Preparedness

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Patient Safety, Triage (Management of Care)

WHAT: Fire safety and emergency preparedness encompass the protocols, procedures, and knowledge needed to respond to fires, natural disasters, mass casualty events, bioterrorism, and other emergencies in the healthcare setting.

WHY IT MATTERS: You've got oxygen running, electrical equipment humming, and chemicals stored down the hall — hospitals are tinderboxes with linoleum. The NCLEX tests fire response and disaster triage because when things go sideways, hesitation costs lives.

MECHANISM:

Fire Response — RACE:
- Rescue: Remove patients from immediate danger
- Alarm: Pull the fire alarm, call the fire code
- Contain: Close doors and windows to contain smoke and fire
- Extinguish: Attempt to extinguish small fires if safe to do so (or Evacuate if not)

Fire Extinguisher — PASS:
- Pull the pin
- Aim at the base of the fire
- Squeeze the handle
- Sweep side to side

Disaster Triage (Mass Casualty Incident):
In a mass casualty event, triage shifts from "do the most for the individual" to "do the most good for the most people." Color-coded triage:
- Red (Immediate): Life-threatening injuries that are survivable with immediate intervention
- Yellow (Delayed): Serious injuries that can wait for treatment without immediate life threat
- Green (Minor): Walking wounded — can wait or help themselves
- Black (Expectant/Deceased): Injuries incompatible with survival given available resources, or deceased

This is the opposite of normal triage: in routine practice, you see the sickest first. In a mass casualty, you prioritize those most likely to survive with intervention — the patient who would normally get all your resources might be triaged as expectant if they'd consume resources that could save five others.

RECOGNIZE:
- Fire hazards: frayed cords, oxygen near open flames, overloaded outlets, blocked exits, improper storage of flammable materials
- Disaster indicators: institutional alerts, influx of patients with similar symptoms (possible bioterrorism or chemical exposure), environmental events
- Bioterrorism agents: anthrax (skin lesions, respiratory distress), smallpox (widespread vesicular rash), botulism (descending paralysis), sarin (miosis, excessive secretions)

ACT:
1. Know your facility's emergency codes and evacuation routes
2. In a fire: RACE, then PASS if attempting to extinguish
3. Close oxygen valves nearest to the fire
4. In disaster triage: triage rapidly, tag patients, direct walking wounded to a separate area
5. Follow the incident command structure — there's a chain of command in every disaster
6. Maintain personal safety — you can't help anyone if you become a casualty

CONNECT:
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Prioritization — normal vs. disaster triage uses different frameworks
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Burn Care, Chemical Exposure
→ See Section 2: Hazardous Materials — proper handling during emergencies

TEST TIP: Fire? RACE — rescue first, then alarm. Disaster triage? The framework flips on you: you're NOT treating the sickest patient first. You're treating the most salvageable. A 90% TBSA burn patient who'd get aggressive treatment on a normal day? In a mass casualty, that's expectant — black tag. This concept is counterintuitive, and the NCLEX knows it. Expect it.

PRACTICE:
During a mass casualty event, a nurse is triaging victims. Which patient should receive care first?
A. A patient with a large open fracture of the femur with controlled bleeding
B. A patient with bilateral arm fractures who is ambulatory
C. A patient with severe burns over 85% of the body and agonal breathing
D. A patient with a penetrating chest wound and labored breathing

Answer: D. The penetrating chest wound patient has a life-threatening but potentially survivable injury requiring immediate intervention (red tag). Option A (yellow — serious but can wait briefly). Option B (green — ambulatory). Option C (black/expectant — this injury severity has an extremely poor prognosis and would consume massive resources). Why D works: In mass casualty triage, the priority goes to patients most likely to survive with immediate intervention.

Medication Safety

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: All pharmacology content, Error Reporting, Documentation

WHAT: Medication safety encompasses the practices, checks, and systems designed to prevent medication errors — from prescribing through administration to monitoring.

WHY IT MATTERS: Med errors are one of the most common ways patients get hurt — and one of the most preventable. The NCLEX tests medication safety relentlessly because every nurse, every shift, handles drugs that can heal or harm depending on whether you got the rights right.

MECHANISM:

Rights of Medication Administration (minimum 6, many institutions use 9–10):
1. Right Patient — use two identifiers (name + DOB or name + medical record number). Never use room number alone.
2. Right Drug — verify the medication matches the order. Check for sound-alike/look-alike drugs.
3. Right Dose — verify the dose is appropriate. Calculate accurately. Question any dose that seems unusual.
4. Right Route — verify the correct route (PO, IV, IM, SubQ, etc.). Some medications are route-specific — giving an IM medication IV can be fatal.
5. Right Time — administer within the acceptable window per facility policy (usually ±30 minutes of scheduled time).
6. Right Documentation — document after administration, not before. Include the medication, dose, route, time, site (for injections), and patient response.
7. Right Reason — why is this patient receiving this medication? If you can't answer that, investigate before giving it.
8. Right Response — monitor for expected therapeutic effects and adverse effects.
9. Right to Refuse — a competent patient can refuse any medication. Document the refusal and notify the provider.

Three Checks:
1. When pulling the medication from storage
2. When preparing the medication
3. At the bedside before administration

High-Alert Medications (increased risk of significant harm if used in error):
- Insulin, heparin/anticoagulants, opioids, neuromuscular blocking agents, concentrated electrolytes (potassium chloride IV), chemotherapy — these require independent double-checks

RECOGNIZE:
- Medication error indicators: wrong drug, wrong dose, wrong route, wrong patient, wrong time, omission
- High-risk situations: verbal/telephone orders (always read back), look-alike/sound-alike drugs, patient transfers (medication reconciliation gaps), multiple concentrations available
- Allergy assessment: always verify allergies before administering any medication — especially antibiotics and NSAIDs

ACT:
1. Verify the order — if it doesn't seem right, clarify with the provider before administering
2. Perform the three checks
3. Use two patient identifiers at the bedside
4. Assess allergies before first-time medications
5. Educate the patient: what the medication is, why they're taking it, what to expect, what to report
6. Monitor for therapeutic and adverse effects
7. Document after administration — never pre-chart
8. For high-alert medications: independent double-check with another nurse
9. For verbal/telephone orders: write the order, read it back, receive confirmation

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2: Error Reporting — what to do when a medication error occurs
→ See Section 5–8 (Physiological Integrity): Specific pharmacology by system
→ See Section 9 (Quick Reference): High-Alert Medication List

TEST TIP: Medication safety questions often hinge on what the nurse does next when something doesn't seem right. If the ordered dose seems too high, the correct answer is never "give the medication and monitor" — it's always "hold the medication and clarify the order." Also: the NCLEX uses the "rights" framework extensively. If any right is violated in the scenario, that's the answer to "which action requires intervention?" The read-back process for telephone orders is also frequently tested.

PRACTICE:
A nurse receives a telephone order from a provider for a medication. Which action by the nurse is most appropriate?
A. Administer the medication and document the verbal order
B. Write down the order, read it back to the provider, and receive confirmation
C. Ask another nurse to listen to the order as a witness
D. Wait until the provider can enter the order electronically before administering

Answer: B. The read-back process is the standard for telephone/verbal orders: write it down, read it back, get confirmation. This reduces transcription errors. Option A skips the verification step. Option C adds a witness but doesn't confirm accuracy through read-back. Option D delays potentially time-sensitive care. Why B works: Write-down and read-back is the established safety protocol for verbal/telephone orders.

Error Reporting & Near Misses

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Quality Improvement, Medication Safety, Documentation, Legal Issues

WHAT: Error reporting is the process of documenting and reporting adverse events, medication errors, near misses (events that almost caused harm but were caught), and any deviations from expected outcomes. The goal is system improvement, not punishment.

WHY IT MATTERS: Unreported errors are the most dangerous kind — if nobody knows about them, nobody can prevent them from happening again. A culture of safety encourages transparent reporting so the system can learn and improve.

MECHANISM:
- Incident/occurrence reports are internal documents used for quality improvement — they are NOT part of the patient's medical record
- What to report: actual errors, near misses, adverse events, equipment malfunctions, falls, injuries, safety hazards
- Who to notify: supervisor/charge nurse immediately, provider (if patient is affected), risk management per facility policy
- What goes in the medical record: objective documentation of the patient's condition, interventions performed, and outcomes — not the error report itself, not blame, not subjective opinions about what went wrong
- Root cause analysis follows significant events — analyzing systems, not blaming individuals

ACT:
1. If a patient is affected: assess and treat the patient first — always
2. Notify the provider if there's an actual or potential impact on the patient
3. Notify the charge nurse/supervisor
4. Complete the incident/occurrence report per facility policy — factual, objective, specific
5. Document in the medical record what happened to the patient and what was done about it (but do NOT reference the incident report in the chart)
6. Participate in any follow-up investigation or root cause analysis
7. Never cover up an error — transparency is both an ethical and a safety imperative

CONNECT:
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Quality Improvement — error reporting feeds QI
→ See Section 2: Medication Safety — medication errors are the most commonly reported events
→ See Section 1: Legal Issues — understanding the legal protections of incident reports

TEST TIP: Four things the NCLEX loves to test on incident reports: (1) the report is NOT part of the medical record, (2) document the patient's condition objectively in the chart — don't mention the incident report, (3) error discovered? First action is always assess the patient, (4) near misses get reported too — no harm doesn't mean no problem. They reveal where the system is cracking.

PRACTICE:
A nurse discovers she administered a medication to the wrong patient. The patient is asymptomatic. What is the nurse's first action?
A. Complete an incident report immediately
B. Assess the patient for any adverse effects
C. Notify the nursing supervisor
D. Document the error in the patient's medical record

Answer: B. Always assess the patient first. Even though the patient is currently asymptomatic, monitoring for adverse effects is the immediate priority. Then notify the provider, notify the supervisor, complete the incident report, and document the patient's condition (not the incident report) in the chart. Why B works: Patient assessment and safety always come first, before administrative tasks.

Patient Identification

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Medication Safety, all procedures

WHAT: Patient identification is the process of verifying a patient's identity before any care activity using at least two approved identifiers. This is a National Patient Safety Goal.

WHY IT MATTERS: Wrong-patient errors affect every aspect of care — medications given to the wrong patient, procedures performed on the wrong patient, lab specimens mislabeled. Two identifiers prevent these errors.

ACT:
1. Use at least two identifiers: typically name and date of birth, or name and medical record number
2. Verify identity before: medication administration, blood transfusions, specimen collection, procedures, treatments, dietary changes
3. Never use room number as an identifier — patients move
4. For patients who cannot state their name (unconscious, intubated, nonverbal): verify the identification band
5. If the ID band is missing or illegible, replace it before performing any care

TEST TIP: The NCLEX may present a scenario where one identifier matches but the other doesn't. The correct answer is always to stop and investigate — never proceed with only one verified identifier.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is about to administer a blood transfusion. The patient's armband reads "Smith, John — DOB 03/15/1950" and the blood product label reads "Smith, John — DOB 03/15/1960." The nurse should:
A. Proceed with the transfusion since the name matches
B. Ask the patient to verify their date of birth before proceeding
C. Hold the transfusion and verify all information with the blood bank
D. Administer the transfusion but monitor closely for reactions

Answer: C. A discrepancy between the patient identifier and the blood product label is a red flag. The nurse must hold the transfusion and verify with the blood bank before proceeding. Even one mismatched identifier means the transfusion cannot proceed until the discrepancy is resolved. Why C works: Blood transfusion errors can be fatal — any discrepancy requires full verification before proceeding.

Equipment Safety, Ergonomics, & Workplace Safety

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Fall Prevention, Infection Control

WHAT: Proper use, maintenance, and safety verification of medical equipment, along with body mechanics and workplace safety principles that protect both patients and staff.

WHY IT MATTERS: A frayed cord shocks a patient. A nurse lifts wrong and blows out a disc. Both are preventable, both are on you to catch. Equipment safety protects patients; body mechanics protect you.

ACT:
1. Inspect equipment before use — check cords, connections, alarms, and calibration
2. Report malfunctioning equipment immediately — remove from use, tag it, and replace
3. Use proper body mechanics: wide base of support, lift with legs (not back), keep the load close to your body, avoid twisting — pivot instead
4. Use mechanical lift devices for patients who cannot bear weight or are above the safe manual lift threshold
5. Lock wheels on beds, wheelchairs, and stretchers before patient transfers
6. Follow electrical safety: no multi-plug adapters near patient care areas, report frayed cords, ground all equipment
7. Know the location and use of emergency equipment on your unit (crash cart, fire extinguisher, suction)
8. Follow facility security protocols: newborn security systems, visitor management, workplace violence prevention plans

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2: Fall Prevention — equipment and environmental safety overlap
→ See Section 2: Error Reporting — equipment malfunctions are reportable events
→ See Section 2: Hazardous Materials — safe handling of biohazardous and chemical materials

TEST TIP: Equipment safety questions love the "which requires intervention?" format — spot the unsafe practice. Frayed cord, ungrounded equipment, alarm silenced, dependent patient moved without a lift device. Body mechanics questions? Look for the obviously wrong answer: bending at the waist, twisting while lifting. They make it clear if you know what to look for.

Hazardous Materials & Biohazardous Waste

Safety and Infection Prevention · Exam Weight: 13% · Connects to: Infection Control, Workplace Safety

WHAT: Proper handling, storage, labeling, and disposal of hazardous materials (chemicals, cytotoxic drugs, radioactive materials) and biohazardous waste (blood, body fluids, sharps, contaminated materials).

WHY IT MATTERS: Hazardous materials aren't optional-knowledge territory — OSHA requires proper handling, and one sloppy exposure can put staff, patients, and the environment at risk. Know the protocols. They're tested because they matter.

ACT:
1. Know the location of Safety Data Sheets (SDS, formerly MSDS) — they contain hazard information, first aid measures, and spill procedures for all chemicals on the unit
2. Use proper PPE when handling hazardous materials
3. Dispose of sharps in puncture-resistant containers at the point of use — never recap needles
4. Use red biohazard bags for contaminated materials
5. Follow specific protocols for chemotherapy drug handling (double gloving, gown, eye protection, specialized disposal)
6. For radiation safety: minimize time near the source, maximize distance, use shielding. Pregnant staff should not care for patients with radiation implants.
7. In case of chemical spill: evacuate the area, consult the SDS, call the appropriate response team — do not attempt to clean up unknown substances yourself

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2: Infection Control — biohazardous waste management is an extension of infection prevention
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Oncology — chemotherapy safety

TEST TIP: Know the three principles of radiation safety: time, distance, shielding. For chemotherapy questions, remember double gloving and that the nurse should not be pregnant. SDS questions test whether you know what they contain and where to find them — not whether you've memorized specific chemical data.

SECTION 3: HEALTH PROMOTION AND MAINTENANCE

Exam Weight: 6–12% (midpoint 9%)

This is the section where you're not fixing what's broken — you're keeping it from breaking in the first place. Prenatal care, developmental milestones, screenings, patient teaching — it's nursing at its most proactive, and the NCLEX wants to know you can think upstream.

Health Promotion represents about one in eleven questions. This section is about wellness, prevention, and life-stage-appropriate care — from preconception through the end of life. It covers obstetrics (a favorite NCLEX topic), pediatric development, immunizations, screening, and healthy aging. If Safety is about preventing bad things, Health Promotion is about building and maintaining the good.

What's in this section: Prenatal Care, Stages of Labor & Delivery, Postpartum Care, Newborn Care & Assessment, Growth & Development (Erikson + Milestones), Immunizations, Health Screening & Disease Prevention, Aging Process.

Prenatal Care

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Stages of Labor, Postpartum, Newborn Care

WHAT: Prenatal care encompasses the assessment, education, and monitoring provided to pregnant clients from conception through the onset of labor, including risk identification, nutrition guidance, screening, and management of pregnancy-related complications.

WHY IT MATTERS: Prenatal care isn't just routine visits — it's the difference between catching preeclampsia at 28 weeks and missing it until seizures start. The NCLEX tests whether you know normal from abnormal, when to educate, and when to escalate, because early intervention changes outcomes for two patients at once.

MECHANISM:

Determining Due Date:
Naegele's Rule: Take the first day of the last menstrual period (LMP), add 7 days, then subtract 3 months. Example: LMP January 10 → January 17 → October 17 (estimated due date).

Pregnancy Tracking — GTPAL:
- Gravida: Total number of pregnancies (including current)
- Term: Number of births at ≥37 weeks
- Preterm: Number of births between 20–36 weeks
- Abortions: Number of pregnancies ending before 20 weeks (spontaneous or elective)
- Living: Number of currently living children

Trimester Overview:
- First trimester (weeks 1–12): Organogenesis occurs — highest risk for teratogenic effects. Nausea/vomiting common. Confirm pregnancy, establish baseline labs, begin prenatal vitamins with folic acid. Ideally, folic acid supplementation begins 1–3 months before conception to prevent neural tube defects (400–800 mcg daily).
- Second trimester (weeks 13–27): Quickening (first fetal movement felt by mother) around 16–20 weeks. Anatomy scan around 18–22 weeks. Glucose screening at 24–28 weeks. The patient typically feels best during this trimester.
- Third trimester (weeks 28–40): Fetal lung maturity developing. Watch for preeclampsia. Group B Strep (GBS) screening at 35–37 weeks. RhoGAM given at 28 weeks for Rh-negative mothers.

Prenatal Danger Signs (Teach Patients to Report Immediately):
- Vaginal bleeding at any time
- Sudden severe headache, visual changes, epigastric pain (preeclampsia warning signs)
- Rupture of membranes before 37 weeks
- Decreased fetal movement
- Signs of infection (fever, chills, dysuria)
- Severe persistent vomiting (hyperemesis gravidarum)
- Edema of face and hands (not just ankle swelling, which is normal)

TORCH Infections — infections that cross the placenta and cause congenital damage:
- Toxoplasmosis (avoid cat litter, undercooked meat)
- Other (syphilis, varicella, parvovirus B19, Zika)
- Rubella (greatest risk in first trimester — causes cardiac defects, cataracts, deafness)
- Cytomegalovirus (most common congenital infection)
- Herpes simplex (risk during vaginal delivery with active lesions → C-section indicated)

Rh Incompatibility:
- Occurs when an Rh-negative mother carries an Rh-positive fetus
- Mother can develop antibodies against fetal Rh-positive blood cells (sensitization), causing hemolytic disease of the newborn in subsequent pregnancies
- Prevention: RhoGAM (Rh immunoglobulin) given at 28 weeks gestation AND within 72 hours after delivery of an Rh-positive infant. Also given after any event that could cause fetal-maternal blood mixing (miscarriage, amniocentesis, abdominal trauma).

RECOGNIZE:
- Normal findings: nausea in first trimester, round ligament pain, Braxton-Hicks contractions (irregular, non-progressive), increased urinary frequency, mild ankle edema
- Abnormal findings: vaginal bleeding, BP ≥140/90, proteinuria, severe headache, visual disturbances, decreased fetal movement, fundal height not matching gestational age (±2 cm is acceptable)

ACT:
1. Establish baseline assessment: weight, BP, urine, labs (blood type, Rh, CBC, STI screening, rubella titer)
2. Educate on nutrition: increased caloric needs (~340 additional kcal/day in 2nd trimester, ~450 in 3rd), adequate protein, iron, calcium, folic acid
3. Screen appropriately: glucose challenge at 24–28 weeks, GBS at 35–37 weeks, genetic screening per patient choice
4. Monitor fetal well-being: fetal heart tones at each visit (normal: 110–160 bpm), kick counts in third trimester
5. Teach danger signs — patients should know what's urgent
6. Address substance use, medication safety (category considerations), and environmental exposures

MEDS:
- Folic acid 400–800 mcg daily: Prevents neural tube defects. Why? Folic acid is essential for neural tube closure, which occurs in the first 4 weeks — often before the patient knows she's pregnant.
- Prenatal vitamins with iron: Support increased blood volume and fetal development. Why? Pregnancy increases iron demands by ~50% to support expanded maternal blood volume and fetal growth.
- RhoGAM: Prevents Rh sensitization. Why? RhoGAM destroys any fetal Rh-positive cells in the mother's circulation before her immune system can mount an antibody response.

CONNECT:
→ See Section 3: Stages of Labor — what happens when prenatal care transitions to delivery
→ See Section 3: Newborn Care — assessment findings that relate to prenatal exposures
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Gestational Diabetes, Preeclampsia/Eclampsia

TEST TIP: The NCLEX frequently tests Naegele's rule — practice the calculation until it's automatic. Know that folic acid should start before conception and that RhoGAM timing (28 weeks + within 72 hours post-delivery) is heavily tested. TORCH infections are a favorite topic — focus on the maternal behaviors that prevent transmission (avoid cat litter, avoid undercooked meat, hand hygiene). For danger signs, the NCLEX often asks "which statement by the patient indicates understanding of when to call the provider?" — the correct answer is the patient who recognizes a danger sign.

PRACTICE:
An Rh-negative pregnant client at 28 weeks gestation asks why she needs the RhoGAM injection. The nurse's best explanation is:
A. "It will prevent you from developing an infection that could harm the baby."
B. "It prevents your body from making antibodies against the baby's blood cells if the baby is Rh-positive."
C. "It boosts your immune system to protect both you and the baby during delivery."
D. "It ensures the baby's blood type is compatible with yours."

Answer: B. RhoGAM contains Rh immunoglobulin that destroys any Rh-positive fetal cells in the mother's circulation before she can develop her own antibodies against them. This protects current and future pregnancies. Why B works: It accurately and clearly explains the mechanism — preventing maternal antibody development against Rh-positive fetal blood.

Stages of Labor & Delivery

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Prenatal Care, Postpartum Care, Newborn Care

WHAT: Labor and delivery progress through four defined stages, each with specific assessments, expected findings, and nursing interventions. Understanding the normal progression allows you to identify deviations that require immediate intervention.

WHY IT MATTERS: Labor complications can become emergencies within minutes. Knowing what's normal lets you spot what's not — and the NCLEX will test your ability to distinguish between expected findings and warning signs.

MECHANISM:

Stage 1: Cervical Dilation and Effacement (longest stage)
- Latent phase: 0–6 cm dilation. Contractions mild to moderate, irregular to regular, 5–30 minutes apart. Patient may be talkative and anxious. Duration is highly variable.
- Active phase: 6–10 cm dilation. Contractions stronger, regular, 3–5 minutes apart, lasting 40–60 seconds. Increasing discomfort, decreased sociability. This is when most patients request pain management.

Stage 2: Delivery of the Baby
- Full dilation (10 cm) through delivery. Patient feels urge to push. Contractions every 2–3 minutes, lasting 60–90 seconds.
- Duration: typically up to 2 hours for primiparas, up to 1 hour for multiparas (longer with epidural).

Stage 3: Delivery of the Placenta
- Occurs within 5–30 minutes after delivery. Signs of placental separation: gush of blood, lengthening of the umbilical cord, uterus becomes firm and globular.
- Inspect placenta for completeness — retained fragments cause hemorrhage.

Stage 4: Recovery (First 1–4 Hours Postpartum)
- Critical monitoring period. Assess: vital signs every 15 minutes × 1 hour, then every 30 minutes × 1 hour. Fundal firmness and position (should be midline at or below the umbilicus). Lochia (amount, color, clots). Bladder distention. Perineal integrity.

Fetal Monitoring — Key Patterns:
- Normal FHR: 110–160 bpm
- Accelerations: Transient increases in FHR — reassuring sign of fetal well-being
- Early Decelerations: Mirror contractions (onset and recovery match contraction pattern). Caused by fetal head compression. Generally benign — no intervention needed.
- Variable Decelerations: Abrupt drops that vary in timing and shape. Caused by cord compression. Intervention: reposition the mother, check for cord prolapse.
- Late Decelerations: Begin after the contraction peaks, recover after the contraction ends. Caused by uteroplacental insufficiency (the placenta isn't delivering enough oxygen). This is ominous — intervene immediately: position on left side, administer oxygen, increase IV fluids, stop oxytocin if infusing, notify the provider.

RECOGNIZE:
- Normal labor progression vs. prolonged labor (failure to progress)
- Reassuring fetal status: baseline 110–160, moderate variability, accelerations present, no late or variable decelerations
- Non-reassuring fetal status: late decelerations, absent variability, prolonged decelerations, fetal tachycardia or bradycardia
- Cord prolapse: sudden variable or prolonged deceleration after membrane rupture, visible or palpable cord

ACT:
1. Monitor contraction pattern: frequency, duration, intensity, resting tone
2. Monitor FHR continuously during active labor
3. Position changes for comfort and optimal fetal perfusion (left lateral preferred)
4. Support hydration and comfort measures
5. For non-reassuring FHR: left lateral position → oxygen → increase IV fluids → stop oxytocin → notify provider (in that order)
6. For cord prolapse: elevate the presenting part off the cord (manual pressure with gloved hand), Trendelenburg or knee-chest position, call for emergency cesarean — do NOT attempt to push the cord back in
7. Assess fundus and bleeding throughout Stage 4

MEDS:
- Oxytocin (Pitocin): Induces or augments labor; also used postpartum to prevent hemorrhage by promoting uterine contraction. Why? Oxytocin stimulates uterine smooth muscle contraction. Requires continuous fetal monitoring during use — overstimulation (tachysystole: >5 contractions in 10 minutes) is the primary risk.
- Magnesium sulfate: Used for preeclampsia seizure prevention and as a tocolytic. Why? It acts as a central nervous system depressant and smooth muscle relaxant. Monitor for toxicity: loss of deep tendon reflexes (first sign), respiratory depression, cardiac arrest. Antidote: calcium gluconate.
- Tocolytics (terbutaline, nifedipine, indomethacin): Suppress preterm labor contractions. Why? Goal is to delay delivery long enough to administer corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity (minimum 48 hours).

CONNECT:
→ See Section 3: Prenatal Care — risk factors identified prenatally affect labor management
→ See Section 3: Postpartum Care — Stage 4 transitions into postpartum
→ See Section 3: Newborn Care — APGAR assessment occurs immediately after delivery
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Preeclampsia/Eclampsia, Placenta Previa, Abruptio Placentae

TEST TIP: Fetal heart rate interpretation is an NCLEX goldmine — drill this framework cold. Accelerations = always reassuring. Early decels = head compression = benign. Variable decels = cord compression = reposition mom. Late decels = placental insufficiency = this is an emergency. When the exam asks "what's the nurse's first action with late decels?" — you're turning that mother onto her left side and getting ready to intervene. And here's the one they love to spring on you: if oxytocin is running and you see tachysystole or a non-reassuring strip, your first move is to stop the pit. Not turn it down. Stop it.

PRACTICE:
A nurse monitoring a laboring client observes a pattern of fetal heart rate decelerations that begin after the peak of contractions and return to baseline after the contraction ends. The nurse should first:
A. Document the finding as a normal variant and continue monitoring
B. Turn the client to the left lateral position and increase IV fluid rate
C. Prepare for immediate cesarean delivery
D. Encourage the client to begin pushing to expedite delivery

Answer: B. Late decelerations indicate uteroplacental insufficiency. The first nursing actions are to improve perfusion: left lateral positioning and increased IV fluids. Then notify the provider. Option A misidentifies late decelerations as normal (they're not — early decelerations are benign, not late). Option C may be needed but isn't the first action. Option D could worsen the situation. Why B works: Improving maternal position and hydration can increase placental perfusion and is the immediate first-line intervention.

Postpartum Care

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Stages of Labor, Newborn Care, Psychosocial (Mood Disorders)

WHAT: Postpartum care covers the assessment, monitoring, education, and support provided to the mother from delivery through approximately 6 weeks post-birth, with a focus on recovery, complication prevention, and family adjustment.

WHY IT MATTERS: Postpartum looks calm from the outside, but it's hiding hemorrhage, infection, thromboembolism, and mood disorders behind that "everything went fine" energy. Your assessments during this window are what catch complications before they spiral — miss the boggy fundus or the creeping tachycardia, and you've lost critical time.

MECHANISM:

Postpartum Assessment — "BUBBLE-HE":
- Breasts: Soft vs. engorged, nipple integrity, breastfeeding assessment
- Uterus: Fundal height and firmness. Should be firm, midline, and descend approximately 1 fingerbreadth per day. A boggy (soft) uterus = hemorrhage risk → massage it.
- Bladder: Monitor for urinary retention (common after epidural). Full bladder displaces uterus, increasing hemorrhage risk.
- Bowels: Assess bowel function return. Constipation common.
- Lochia: Normal progression — rubra (red, days 1–3) → serosa (pinkish-brown, days 4–10) → alba (white/yellow, days 10–14+). Report: saturating a pad in <1 hour, foul odor (infection), return to rubra after serosa (possible retained fragments).
- Episiotomy/perineum/cesarean incision: Assess healing, signs of infection (redness, swelling, drainage, separation), hematoma.
- Homan's sign/lower extremities: Assess for DVT risk (edema, calf tenderness, warmth, redness). Note: Homan's sign has low specificity but DVT assessment remains important.
- Emotions: Assess for postpartum blues (days 3–14, mild, self-limiting) vs. postpartum depression (persistent, more severe, onset within first year, may require treatment) vs. postpartum psychosis (rare, psychiatric emergency — hallucinations, delusions, thoughts of harming self or infant).

RECOGNIZE:
- Hemorrhage indicators: boggy uterus, excessive bleeding (>1 pad/hour), large clots, hypotension, tachycardia
- Infection indicators: temperature >100.4°F (38°C) after first 24 hours, foul-smelling lochia, uterine tenderness, wound drainage
- DVT indicators: unilateral calf pain, swelling, redness, warmth
- Postpartum mood disorder indicators: persistent sadness beyond 2 weeks, inability to care for self or infant, psychotic symptoms (hallucinations, delusions)

ACT:
1. Assess uterine fundus regularly — if boggy, massage firmly until firm. If it doesn't firm with massage, notify the provider.
2. Monitor lochia: amount, color, odor, clots
3. Encourage early ambulation (reduces DVT risk, promotes recovery)
4. Support breastfeeding: proper latch, feeding frequency (8–12 times/day for newborns), signs of adequate intake (wet diapers, weight gain)
5. Educate on perineal care: ice first 24 hours, sitz baths after 24 hours, peri-bottle use, wipe front to back
6. Screen for postpartum depression using validated tools (Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale)
7. Educate on contraception, activity restrictions, and when to call the provider
8. RhoGAM within 72 hours if indicated (Rh-negative mother, Rh-positive infant)

MEDS:
- Oxytocin (Pitocin) IV: Promotes uterine contraction postpartum. Why? A contracted uterus compresses placental blood vessels, preventing hemorrhage.
- Methylergonovine (Methergine): Sustained uterine contraction for hemorrhage management. Why? Causes intense, prolonged uterine contraction. Contraindicated in hypertension — check BP before administering.
- Stool softeners (docusate sodium): Prevent straining. Why? Straining increases pain and risk of episiotomy/hemorrhoid complications.
- Rh immunoglobulin (RhoGAM): If indicated, within 72 hours postpartum. Why? Prevents Rh sensitization before the next pregnancy.

CONNECT:
→ See Section 3: Stages of Labor — Stage 4 is the bridge to postpartum
→ See Section 3: Newborn Care — maternal and newborn care happen simultaneously
→ See Section 4 (Psychosocial): Postpartum Depression — mood disorders in detail

TEST TIP: A boggy uterus question appears on virtually every NCLEX. Know this sequence: find it boggy → massage → if it firms, continue monitoring → if it doesn't firm, notify the provider. Also know that a full bladder can prevent the uterus from contracting — so if the fundus is boggy and displaced to one side, have the patient void first. Lochia color progression (rubra → serosa → alba) is tested directly. Postpartum hemorrhage questions may ask about Methergine — remember to check BP first (contraindicated in hypertension).

PRACTICE:
A nurse assessing a client 2 hours postpartum finds the uterine fundus is boggy and displaced to the right of midline. The first nursing action is:
A. Massage the fundus firmly until it becomes firm
B. Assist the client to void and then reassess the fundus
C. Administer oxytocin as ordered
D. Notify the provider of the abnormal finding

Answer: B. A fundus displaced to the right often indicates a distended bladder pushing the uterus to the side. The first action is to empty the bladder, which often allows the uterus to contract and return to midline. If the fundus remains boggy after voiding, then massage and medicate. Why B works: Addressing the most likely cause (full bladder) first is more effective than massaging a uterus that will continue to be displaced by the underlying problem.

Newborn Care & Assessment

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Prenatal Care, Postpartum, Growth & Development

WHAT: Newborn care covers the immediate assessment (APGAR), transitional period, physical examination, routine care (vitamin K, eye prophylaxis, screening), feeding, and parental education during the first month of life.

WHY IT MATTERS: Newborns can't tell you something's wrong — they just deteriorate. Your rapid assessment is the safety net between a normal transition and a life-threatening emergency. The NCLEX wants to know you can look at a newborn and tell the difference between "this is fine" and "this needs intervention right now."

MECHANISM:

APGAR Scoring — performed at 1 minute and 5 minutes after birth:

Sign 0 1 2
Appearance (color) Blue/pale all over Body pink, extremities blue (acrocyanosis) Completely pink
Pulse (heart rate) Absent <100 bpm ≥100 bpm
Grimace (reflex irritability) No response Grimace Cry, cough, sneeze
Activity (muscle tone) Limp Some flexion Active movement
Respiration Absent Slow, irregular, weak cry Strong cry
  • Score 7–10: Normal — routine care
  • Score 4–6: Moderate depression — stimulation, possible airway support
  • Score 0–3: Severe depression — immediate resuscitation

Normal Newborn Findings (Don't Be Alarmed):
- Acrocyanosis (bluish hands/feet in first 24–48 hours — normal peripheral circulation adaptation)
- Milia (tiny white bumps on face — blocked sebaceous glands, resolve spontaneously)
- Mongolian spots (blue-gray pigmented areas on sacral region, common in darker-skinned infants — document to distinguish from bruising)
- Erythema toxicum (blotchy red rash with small white/yellow papules — newborn rash, benign, resolves)
- Epstein's pearls (small white cysts on palate — normal)
- Molding (elongated head shape from vaginal delivery — resolves within days)
- Caput succedaneum (soft tissue swelling of scalp, crosses suture lines, resolves quickly)
- Cephalohematoma (blood collection between skull bone and periosteum, does NOT cross suture lines, resolves over weeks to months)

Abnormal Findings (Intervene):
- Central cyanosis (blue trunk, face — indicates hypoxia)
- Respiratory distress: grunting, nasal flaring, retractions, tachypnea (>60/min)
- Jitteriness vs. seizure activity
- Hypoglycemia (blood glucose <40 mg/dL in first hours) — feed immediately if alert, IV dextrose if symptomatic
- Temperature instability — hypothermia is a significant risk for newborns
- Absent or weak cry, poor muscle tone

Routine Newborn Care:
- Vitamin K (phytonadione) injection within 1 hour of birth — prevents hemorrhagic disease of the newborn. Why? Newborns have sterile guts and cannot synthesize vitamin K (needed for clotting factors) until gut flora is established.
- Erythromycin eye ointment — prevents ophthalmia neonatorum (gonococcal/chlamydial eye infection). Why? Applied prophylactically because maternal STIs may be present even without symptoms.
- Hepatitis B vaccine — first dose within 24 hours of birth
- Newborn metabolic screening (heel stick) — typically performed after 24 hours of age. Screens for PKU, hypothyroidism, galactosemia, sickle cell disease, and other conditions depending on state.
- Daily weight, intake/output monitoring, jaundice assessment

RECOGNIZE:
- Normal vital signs: HR 120–160 bpm (may be 100 during sleep, up to 180 briefly when crying), RR 30–60 breaths/min, temperature 97.7–99.5°F (36.5–37.5°C)
- Adequate feeding indicators: 6–8 wet diapers/day by day 4, 3–4 stools/day, regains birth weight by 10–14 days
- Weight loss up to 5-10% of birth weight in first few days is normal

ACT:
1. Perform APGAR at 1 and 5 minutes
2. Maintain thermoregulation: dry immediately, skin-to-skin, warm blankets, hat
3. Administer vitamin K and eye prophylaxis per protocol
4. Assess feeding readiness and support breastfeeding (latch, position, frequency)
5. Monitor for jaundice (especially after 24 hours — pathological if within first 24 hours)
6. Educate parents: safe sleep (back to sleep, firm flat surface, no loose bedding), feeding cues, when to call the provider, car seat safety
7. Newborn security: ID bands, security systems, never leave unattended, verify identity of anyone taking the infant

CONNECT:
→ See Section 3: Prenatal Care — prenatal exposures affect newborn assessment findings
→ See Section 3: Growth & Development — newborn period is the foundation
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Neonatal Jaundice, Congenital Heart Defects
→ See Section 2 (Safety): Newborn Security — identification and security protocols

TEST TIP: APGAR comes up as both math and meaning — 1-minute score tells you "do we resuscitate now?" while the 5-minute score tells you "is what we did working?" The classic mix-up: caput vs. cephalohematoma. Caput crosses suture lines and fades fast; cephalohematoma respects suture lines and sticks around. Vitamin K is a triple-tested favorite — given within 1 hour, by injection (not oral), because the baby's gut has zero flora to make it yet. Hypoglycemia decision tree: baby alert? Feed them. Baby symptomatic? IV dextrose. Don't overthink it.

PRACTICE:
A newborn delivered vaginally has a heart rate of 130, is crying vigorously, has active movement, has a pink body with blue extremities, and grimaces when the nasal bulb syringe is used. What is this newborn's APGAR score?
A. 7
B. 8
C. 9
D. 10

Answer: B. HR 130 = 2 (≥100). Strong cry = 2. Active movement = 2. Pink body/blue extremities (acrocyanosis) = 1. Grimace = 1. Total = 8. Full points for everything except color (acrocyanosis = 1) and reflex (grimace without full cry response to stimulus = 1). Why B works: Systematic scoring: 2+2+2+1+1 = 8.

Growth & Development

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Pediatric Care, Psychosocial (all age groups)

WHAT: Growth and development covers the predictable patterns of physical, cognitive, and psychosocial development across the lifespan, with emphasis on Erikson's psychosocial stages, age-specific milestones, and the nurse's role in screening for developmental delays.

WHY IT MATTERS: A kid who's not rolling, babbling, or walking when they should be isn't just "a late bloomer" — that's a potential red flag that demands follow-up. The NCLEX expects you to know what's on track for each developmental stage and what warrants action, because early intervention changes outcomes.

MECHANISM:

Erikson's Psychosocial Stages:

Age Stage Central Task Key Nursing Implication
Infant (0–1 yr) Trust vs. Mistrust Consistency, responsive caregiving Meet needs promptly, consistent caregivers
Toddler (1–3 yr) Autonomy vs. Shame/Doubt Independence, choices Offer limited choices, allow safe exploration
Preschool (3–6 yr) Initiative vs. Guilt Purpose, creativity Encourage play, answer questions, allow role-playing
School-age (6–12 yr) Industry vs. Inferiority Competence, achievement Praise accomplishments, support skill development
Adolescent (12–18 yr) Identity vs. Role Confusion Self-identity Respect privacy, support peer relationships, allow decision-making
Young adult (18–35 yr) Intimacy vs. Isolation Close relationships Support relationship building
Middle adult (35–65 yr) Generativity vs. Stagnation Contribution to society Support career/family goals
Older adult (65+ yr) Integrity vs. Despair Life review, meaning Facilitate reminiscence, validate life experiences

Key Developmental Milestones (High-Yield for NCLEX):

Infant Milestones:
- 2 months: Social smile, lifts head prone
- 4 months: Rolls front to back, reaches for objects, laughs
- 6 months: Sits with support, transfers objects hand to hand, babbles, stranger anxiety begins
- 9 months: Sits independently, crawls, pincer grasp developing, says "dada/mama" nonspecifically
- 12 months: Pulls to stand, may walk, 1–2 words with meaning, pincer grasp refined

Toddler Milestones (1–3 years):
- 15 months: Walks independently, 3–5 words, stacks 2 blocks
- 18 months: Runs clumsily, uses cup, 10–20 words, points to body parts
- 2 years: Runs well, 2-word phrases ("want milk"), kicks a ball, parallel play
- 3 years: Rides tricycle, 3-word sentences, knows age, copies a circle

Preschool (3–6 years):
- Hops, skips, catches a ball
- Knows colors, counts, tells stories
- Associative play progressing to cooperative play
- Magical thinking — may believe illness is punishment
- Fear of body mutilation — important for procedure preparation

School-Age (6–12 years):
- Logical thinking (Piaget's concrete operations)
- Develops peer relationships, team sports
- Understands cause and effect
- Can understand illness explanations using concrete terms

Adolescent (12–18 years):
- Abstract thinking (Piaget's formal operations)
- Peer identity is paramount
- Body image concerns
- Risk-taking behaviors
- Need for privacy and confidentiality (within legal limits)

Toy and Activity Safety Principles:
- Under 3 years: no small parts (choking hazard — anything fitting through a toilet paper roll is too small)
- No balloons for young children (aspiration risk)
- Age-appropriate toys support the developmental task of the stage

RECOGNIZE:
- Red flags for developmental delay: not babbling by 12 months, no single words by 16 months, not walking by 18 months, loss of previously acquired skills at any age, not making eye contact
- Regression under stress is normal in children (a toilet-trained 4-year-old may wet the bed during hospitalization — this is expected)

ACT:
1. Assess development at each well-child visit using validated screening tools
2. Educate parents on age-appropriate expectations — what's normal, what to watch for
3. Provide age-appropriate explanations for procedures (toddlers: brief, immediately before; preschoolers: simple terms, 1–2 hours before; school-age: concrete explanations, advance preparation; adolescents: detailed explanations, privacy, peer connection)
4. Support play as therapy during hospitalization — play is the work of children
5. Refer for early intervention when delays are identified

CONNECT:
→ See Section 4 (Psychosocial): Therapeutic Communication — age-specific communication strategies
→ See Section 3: Immunizations — well-child visits combine development screening with vaccination
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Pediatric-specific clinical content

TEST TIP: Erikson's stages are tested frequently. The trick is knowing the nursing implication of each stage, not just the name. If a question asks about a hospitalized toddler, think autonomy — offer choices. If it asks about a school-age child, think industry — let them participate in their care. Milestone questions typically present a developmental finding and ask whether it's age-appropriate. If an 18-month-old isn't walking, that's a concern. If a 12-month-old isn't walking, that's still within normal limits.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is planning care for a hospitalized 4-year-old. Which action best supports this child's developmental needs?
A. Explain procedures using detailed anatomical terms
B. Encourage the child to play with medical equipment before procedures
C. Limit parental visits to promote independence
D. Assign a different nurse each shift to build flexibility

Answer: B. Preschoolers learn through play and fear body mutilation. Medical play allows them to explore equipment in a non-threatening way, reducing fear. Option A is developmentally inappropriate (use simple terms). Option C is harmful — preschoolers need parental presence (Erikson: initiative, but still dependent). Option D undermines trust (consistent caregivers support trust, relevant from infancy forward). Why B works: Play is the primary vehicle for understanding and coping at this developmental stage.

Immunizations

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Infection Control, Prenatal Care, Growth & Development

WHAT: Immunizations are biological preparations that stimulate active immunity against specific infectious diseases. The immunization schedule spans from birth through adulthood, with specific timing for each vaccine.

WHY IT MATTERS: Immunizations prevent communicable diseases that once caused widespread morbidity and mortality. The NCLEX tests your knowledge of the schedule, contraindications, and patient education — not detailed immunology.

MECHANISM:
- Active immunity: Body produces its own antibodies in response to vaccine antigens. Takes time to develop but provides long-lasting protection. This is what vaccines create.
- Passive immunity: Preformed antibodies are transferred from another source (maternal antibodies through placenta/breast milk, immunoglobulin injections). Provides immediate but temporary protection.
- Live attenuated vaccines (MMR, varicella, live influenza nasal spray, rotavirus): Contain weakened organisms. Contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and pregnancy.
- Inactivated/killed vaccines (influenza injection, hepatitis A/B, IPV): Contain killed organisms or components. Safe for immunocompromised patients.

Key Schedule Points (NCLEX High-Yield):
- Birth: Hepatitis B (dose 1)
- 2 months: DTaP, IPV, Hib, PCV13, rotavirus, Hep B (dose 2)
- 12–15 months: MMR (dose 1), varicella (dose 1), Hep A (dose 1)
- 4–6 years: DTaP, IPV, MMR (dose 2), varicella (dose 2) — school-entry boosters
- 11–12 years: Tdap, HPV, meningococcal (MenACWY)
- Annual: Influenza (starting at 6 months of age, yearly for everyone ≥6 months)
- 65+ years: Pneumococcal (PCV20 or PCV15+PPSV23), Shingrix (recombinant zoster, 2 doses at age 50+)

Contraindications:
- Severe allergic reaction (anaphylaxis) to a previous dose or vaccine component — absolute contraindication for that vaccine
- Live vaccines: contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and during pregnancy
- Moderate to severe illness with fever — defer until recovered (mild illness with or without low-grade fever is NOT a contraindication)

RECOGNIZE:
- Common reactions (expected): Soreness at injection site, low-grade fever, fussiness — these are immune responses, not reasons to withhold future doses
- Reportable reactions: High fever (>104°F/40°C), seizures, anaphylaxis, persistent inconsolable crying >3 hours — report to the Vaccine Adverse Event Reporting System (VAERS)
- NOT contraindications (common parental misconceptions): mild illness, current antibiotic therapy, recent exposure to illness, prematurity, family history of reactions, breastfeeding

ACT:
1. Review immunization history at every healthcare encounter
2. Educate parents on the schedule, expected side effects, and when to seek care
3. Administer vaccines using proper technique (IM for most, SubQ for some, oral for rotavirus)
4. Observe for anaphylaxis for 15–30 minutes post-vaccination
5. Document: vaccine name, lot number, expiration date, manufacturer, injection site, route, administrator
6. For adolescents: educate about HPV vaccine for cancer prevention

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2 (Safety): Infection Control — immunizations as the primary prevention of vaccine-preventable diseases
→ See Section 3: Growth & Development — immunizations are part of well-child care
→ See Section 3: Prenatal Care — rubella immunity, influenza and Tdap during pregnancy

TEST TIP: The NCLEX won't ask you to recite the entire schedule, but will test critical timing (Hep B at birth, MMR at 12 months — not before), contraindications (live vaccines and immunocompromise/pregnancy), and parent education (mild illness is NOT a reason to delay). A common question format: "which statement by the parent indicates a need for further teaching?" The wrong answer is the parent who thinks a runny nose means they should skip the appointment.

PRACTICE:
A parent brings their 12-month-old for scheduled immunizations. The child has a mild runny nose and is playful. The parent asks, "Should we wait until the cold is gone?" The nurse's best response is:
A. "Yes, it's safer to wait until all symptoms have resolved."
B. "A mild illness is not a reason to delay immunizations. We can give the vaccines today."
C. "We should postpone vaccines until the child is fever-free for 48 hours."
D. "Let me check with the provider to see if we should wait."

Answer: B. A mild upper respiratory infection without fever is NOT a contraindication to vaccination. Delaying unnecessarily creates gaps in immunity. The nurse should educate the parent that mild illness is safe for vaccination. Why B works: Current immunization guidelines clearly state that mild illness is not a contraindication — delaying creates risk without benefit.

Health Screening & Disease Prevention

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: All clinical sections, Aging Process

WHAT: Health screening involves the systematic use of tests and assessments to identify disease or risk factors in asymptomatic individuals. Disease prevention includes primary prevention (preventing disease before it occurs), secondary prevention (early detection), and tertiary prevention (limiting disability from existing disease).

WHY IT MATTERS: Catching disease early — or preventing it entirely — saves lives. The NCLEX tests whether you know which screenings are recommended, when, and how to educate patients about risk reduction.

MECHANISM:
Three Levels of Prevention:
- Primary: Preventing disease from occurring — immunizations, health education, seat belt use, smoking cessation, exercise, proper nutrition
- Secondary: Early detection and treatment — screening tests (mammography, colonoscopy, blood pressure checks, blood glucose monitoring, Pap smears, PSA)
- Tertiary: Reducing complications and disability from existing disease — cardiac rehabilitation after MI, physical therapy after stroke, diabetic foot care

Key Screening Recommendations (NCLEX High-Yield):
- Blood pressure: at every healthcare encounter for adults
- Cholesterol: starting at age 20, every 4–6 years (more frequent if risk factors present)
- Colorectal cancer: screening beginning at age 45 (colonoscopy every 10 years, or alternative methods at different intervals)
- Breast cancer: mammography recommendations vary by guideline (generally starting at age 40–50, annually or biennially). Teach breast self-awareness.
- Cervical cancer: Pap smear starting at age 21 (every 3 years age 21–29; every 3–5 years age 30–65 with HPV co-testing)
- Diabetes: fasting glucose or HbA1c beginning at age 35, or earlier with risk factors (obesity, family history, gestational diabetes history)
- Osteoporosis: DEXA scan at age 65 for women (earlier with risk factors)

Risk Factor Education:
- Modifiable: smoking, obesity, sedentary lifestyle, diet, excessive alcohol, unprotected sex, medication nonadherence
- Non-modifiable: age, sex, family history, genetics, race/ethnicity

ACT:
1. Assess individual risk factors and recommend appropriate screenings
2. Educate patients on screening schedules appropriate to their age and risk profile
3. Teach self-examination awareness (skin checks, breast awareness, testicular awareness)
4. Promote lifestyle modifications: smoking cessation, balanced nutrition, regular exercise, stress management, moderate alcohol use
5. Ensure follow-up on abnormal screening results

CONNECT:
→ See Section 3: Immunizations — primary prevention
→ See Section 3: Aging Process — age-specific screening needs
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Specific disease processes and their screenings

TEST TIP: The NCLEX loves handing you a nursing action and asking you to name the prevention level. Here's your cheat code — teaching a diabetic about foot care? Tertiary. Performing a mammogram? Secondary. Teaching a teenager about safe sex? Primary. Lock those in. And remember: your role in screening is to recommend, educate, and follow up. You don't diagnose — that's not your lane.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is planning a community health education event. Which activity represents primary prevention?
A. Offering blood pressure screenings for adults over 40
B. Teaching adolescents about the risks of tobacco use
C. Providing diabetes management education for diagnosed diabetics
D. Performing vision screenings at an elementary school

Answer: B. Teaching adolescents to avoid tobacco prevents disease from occurring — this is primary prevention. Options A and D are screening (secondary prevention). Option C is disease management (tertiary prevention). Why B works: Primary prevention targets healthy individuals to prevent disease onset entirely.

Aging Process

Health Promotion and Maintenance · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: All sections — older adults are a major patient population

WHAT: The aging process involves predictable physiological, cognitive, and psychosocial changes that occur with advancing age. Understanding normal aging vs. pathological processes is essential for providing appropriate care to older adults.

WHY IT MATTERS: Older adults fill your hospital beds — and if you can't tell the difference between normal aging and actual disease, you'll either miss a real problem or chase a normal finding down a rabbit hole. Both are bad nursing. Both show up on the NCLEX.

MECHANISM:

Normal Age-Related Changes:
- Cardiovascular: Decreased cardiac output, stiffened blood vessels, increased systolic BP, slower heart rate recovery after exertion
- Respiratory: Decreased lung elasticity, reduced vital capacity, diminished cough reflex
- Musculoskeletal: Decreased bone density, reduced muscle mass (sarcopenia), decreased flexibility, changes in balance and gait
- Neurological: Slower processing speed, mild forgetfulness (not dementia), decreased reaction time, reduced deep tendon reflexes
- Sensory: Presbyopia (decreased near vision), presbycusis (decreased hearing — especially high-frequency sounds), diminished taste and smell
- Integumentary: Thinning skin, decreased elasticity, decreased subcutaneous fat, slower wound healing
- Renal: Decreased GFR, reduced ability to concentrate urine, increased susceptibility to drug toxicity (decreased clearance)
- GI: Decreased gastric motility, reduced absorption of some nutrients, increased constipation risk
- Immune: Diminished immune response, increased susceptibility to infection, decreased vaccine response

RECOGNIZE:
- Normal aging: gradual onset, functional ability maintained with adaptation
- Pathological: rapid onset of confusion (delirium, NOT normal aging), significant weight loss, falls, incontinence as a new symptom, depression (not a normal part of aging)
- Polypharmacy risks: older adults often take multiple medications, increasing drug interaction and adverse effect risk

ACT:
1. Assess functional status: ADLs and IADLs — focus on what the patient CAN do
2. Medication review: assess for polypharmacy, inappropriate medications (Beers Criteria), and renal dosing needs
3. Fall prevention: addressed extensively in Section 2
4. Sensory accommodations: adequate lighting, face the patient when speaking, speak clearly at a lower pitch (not louder)
5. Nutritional support: adequate protein, calcium, vitamin D, hydration
6. Cognitive screening when indicated — but don't confuse normal forgetfulness with dementia
7. Promote social engagement — isolation is a significant health risk in older adults
8. Respect autonomy — aging doesn't diminish decision-making capacity unless demonstrated otherwise

CONNECT:
→ See Section 2 (Safety): Fall Prevention — older adults are the highest-risk population
→ See Section 4 (Psychosocial): End-of-Life, Grief & Loss, Coping Mechanisms
→ See Section 5 (Physiological Integrity): Geriatric pharmacology, chronic disease management

TEST TIP: The exam loves blurring the line between aging and disease — your job is to draw it back. Mild forgetfulness? Normal. Acute confusion? That's delirium, and it's never normal. Gradual hearing loss? Expected. Sudden hearing loss? Workup, now. Drug dosing in older adults almost always means lower dose, more monitoring — decreased liver and kidney function slows everything down. And don't fall for the trap: depression in older adults is NOT a normal part of aging. The NCLEX will test you on that distinction specifically.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is assessing a 78-year-old patient. Which finding should the nurse report to the provider as potentially abnormal?
A. Mild difficulty hearing high-pitched sounds
B. Taking longer to recall names of acquaintances
C. New onset of confusion that developed over the past 24 hours
D. Needing reading glasses for close-up work

Answer: C. Acute onset confusion is NEVER normal aging — it suggests delirium, which is a medical emergency requiring investigation (infection, medication effect, metabolic imbalance). Options A (presbycusis), B (normal age-related memory), and D (presbyopia) are all expected age-related changes. Why C is concerning: Acute confusion in an older adult is delirium until proven otherwise — it has an underlying cause that must be identified and treated.

SECTION 4: PSYCHOSOCIAL INTEGRITY

Exam Weight: 6–12% (midpoint 9%)

If Management of Care is about who does what, Psychosocial Integrity is about how you connect with the person in front of you. This is where nursing becomes an art — reading between the lines, holding space, and knowing that sometimes the most therapeutic thing you can do is shut up and listen.

Psychosocial Integrity represents about one in eleven questions. This section covers the emotional, mental, and social dimensions of patient care — from therapeutic communication to mental health disorders, from grief and loss to crisis intervention. If Management of Care is about who does what, Psychosocial Integrity is about how you connect with the person in front of you. This is where nursing is an art, not just a science.

What's in this section: Therapeutic Communication, Mental Health Disorders, Substance Use Disorders, Grief & Loss, Crisis Intervention, Abuse & Neglect, Coping Mechanisms, Cultural & Spiritual Considerations, End-of-Life Care.

Therapeutic Communication

Psychosocial Integrity · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: All sections — communication is the foundation of nursing

WHAT: Therapeutic communication is purposeful, goal-directed interaction that promotes the patient's well-being and facilitates expression of feelings, concerns, and needs. It's not casual conversation — it's a clinical skill.

WHY IT MATTERS: Communication failures are behind the majority of sentinel events in healthcare — not drug errors, not missed labs, communication. The NCLEX hammers therapeutic communication because how you talk to patients directly shapes whether they live, heal, and trust you enough to tell you what's actually going on.

MECHANISM:

Therapeutic Techniques:
- Active listening: Full attention, body language, no interrupting. This is the foundation.
- Open-ended questions: "Tell me about your pain" rather than "Is your pain a 5?" Opens the conversation rather than closing it.
- Reflection: Repeating the emotional content back — "It sounds like you're feeling scared about the surgery."
- Restating/paraphrasing: Echoing the content in your own words to confirm understanding.
- Clarifying: "Can you tell me more about what you mean by that?"
- Silence: Allowing the patient time to think and respond. Silence is therapeutic — don't rush to fill it.
- Focusing: Guiding the conversation back to the relevant topic when it wanders.
- Validation: Acknowledging the patient's feelings as real and important — "It makes sense that you'd feel anxious about this."
- Offering self: "I'll stay with you during the procedure if you'd like."
- Summarizing: Reviewing key points of the conversation to ensure mutual understanding.

Non-Therapeutic Responses (Avoid These):
- "Why?" — sounds accusatory, puts the patient on the defensive
- False reassurance: "Everything will be fine" / "Don't worry" — dismisses legitimate concerns and may be untrue
- "I understand" or "I know how you feel" — you may not, and it can feel dismissive
- Giving advice: "If I were you, I'd..." — undermines patient autonomy
- Changing the subject: Avoiding the patient's concern when it's uncomfortable
- Clichés: "It's God's plan" / "Time heals all wounds" — minimizes the patient's experience
- Approval/disapproval: Judging the patient's feelings or choices
- Closed-ended questions when exploration is needed

Communication Adaptations by Age:
- Infants/Toddlers: Touch, gentle tone, parental presence, play
- Preschoolers: Simple words, pictures, medical play, concrete explanations
- School-age: Logical explanations, involve in care decisions, honest answers
- Adolescents: Respect privacy, direct conversation to them (not just parents), acknowledge autonomy
- Older adults: Face them, speak clearly at a lower pitch, allow time to respond, don't assume cognitive impairment

RECOGNIZE:
- Patient is not expressing their concerns — may need a more open approach
- Barriers to communication: language, hearing impairment, cognitive impairment, pain, fear, cultural differences, power dynamics
- Non-verbal cues: body language often tells you more than words

ACT:
1. Use therapeutic techniques consistently — not just when it's easy
2. Match your communication style to the patient's developmental level, cultural context, and emotional state
3. Be present — put down the chart, make eye contact (if culturally appropriate), sit at the patient's level
4. When a patient expresses fear, sadness, or anger, reflect the emotion before problem-solving
5. Avoid the common traps: false reassurance, advice-giving, clichés, "why?"
6. Use interpreters for patients with language barriers — not family members for medical discussions

CONNECT:
→ See Section 1 (Management of Care): Documentation — documenting patient communication
→ See Section 4: Mental Health Disorders — communication with patients in psychiatric distress
→ See Section 4: Crisis Intervention — communication in crisis situations
→ See Section 3 (Health Promotion): Growth & Development — developmental communication

TEST TIP: Therapeutic communication is the psychosocial topic the NCLEX can't get enough of. The winning answer almost always: (1) explores feelings, (2) is open-ended, (3) shows active listening, and (4) avoids advice or false reassurance. See "Everything will be fine" or "You shouldn't feel that way" in the answer choices? Kill those immediately — they're traps dressed up as kindness. The therapeutic response is the one that keeps the door open for the patient to keep talking. And here's the rule that trips everyone up: when a patient is expressing an emotion, address the emotion first. Not the medical content. The feeling comes before the fact.

PRACTICE:
A patient scheduled for cardiac surgery tells the nurse, "I'm not sure I'm going to make it through this." The nurse's best response is:
A. "The surgeon has an excellent success rate. You're in good hands."
B. "Don't worry, many patients feel anxious before surgery."
C. "It sounds like you have some fears about the surgery. Tell me more about what you're feeling."
D. "Would you like me to call the chaplain?"

Answer: C. This response reflects the patient's emotion (fear) and uses an open-ended invitation to explore further. Option A is false reassurance. Option B dismisses the feeling with "don't worry." Option D may be appropriate later but deflects from the immediate emotional exploration. Why C works: It validates the patient's feelings and encourages them to express their concerns — the core of therapeutic communication.

Mental Health Disorders: Depression

Psychosocial Integrity · Exam Weight: 9% · Connects to: Therapeutic Communication, Pharmacology, Crisis Intervention

WHAT: Major depressive disorder is a mood disorder characterized by persistent, pervasive sadness, loss of interest or pleasure (anhedonia), and associated cognitive, behavioral, and physiological symptoms lasting at least 2 weeks.

WHY IT MATTERS: Depression doesn't just make people sad — it tanks recovery, wrecks medication adherence, and torpedoes quality of life. It's the most common psychiatric disorder, it shows up alongside almost every medical condition, and when you miss it in a hospitalized patient, outcomes get measurably worse.

RECOGNIZE:
- Depressed mood most of the day, nearly every day
- Anhedonia — loss of interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities
- Sleep disturbances (insomnia or hypersomnia)
- Appetite/weight changes (increase or decrease)
- Fatigue, low energy
- Feelings of worthlessness or excessive guilt
- Difficulty concentrating, indecisiveness
- Psychomotor agitation or retardation
- Recurrent thoughts of death or suicidal ideation
- In older adults: depression may present as somatic complaints, cognitive changes (pseudodementia), or social withdrawal rather than expressed sadness

ACT:
1. Screen for depression using validated tools (PHQ-9, Geriatric Depression Scale)
2. Assess for safety — screen for suicidal ideation directly and without hesitation. Ask: "Are you thinking about hurting yourself?"
3. Establish a therapeutic relationship — consistent, nonjudgmental, patient
4. Promote structure: regular activities, sleep hygiene, nutrition, exercise
5. Encourage expression of feelings — don't try to "fix" the depression with cheerfulness
6. Monitor medication effectiveness and side effects (onset may take 2–6 weeks for full effect)
