Rapid correction → osmotic demyelination syndrome (central pontine myelinolysis) → permanent neurological damage. Monitor neuro status. Seizure precautions. Daily weights.

Hypernatremia (Na+ >145 mEq/L)
Causes: dehydration (most common — water loss exceeds sodium loss), diabetes insipidus (massive water loss), excessive sodium intake, fever, burns, profuse sweating without water replacement
Signs — "Cells shrink as water is pulled out": intense thirst, dry/sticky mucous membranes, flushed skin, altered mental status, agitation → seizures (brain cells shrink → tearing of bridging veins possible), low-grade fever, oliguria
Nursing Actions: replace free water (hypotonic fluids: 0.45% NS, D5W). Correct slowly — same principle as hyponatremia, rapid correction → cerebral edema. Monitor neuro status. I&O, daily weights. Safety precautions for confused patients.

CALCIUM IMBALANCES

Hypocalcemia (Ca²+ <8.6 mg/dL)
Causes: hypoparathyroidism (post-thyroidectomy — parathyroids accidentally removed), renal failure (↑phos → ↓Ca), vitamin D deficiency, pancreatitis (fat necrosis binds calcium), massive blood transfusion (citrate in stored blood binds calcium)
Signs — "Everything is hyperexcitable": numbness/tingling (perioral, fingers), muscle cramps/spasms, tetany, seizures, laryngospasm, hyperactive reflexes, positive Trousseau's sign (BP cuff → carpal spasm), positive Chvostek's sign (tap facial nerve → facial twitch), QT prolongation on EKG (↑arrhythmia risk)
Nursing Actions: IV calcium gluconate (slow push — rapid can cause cardiac arrest). Seizure precautions. Place tracheostomy tray at bedside (laryngospasm risk). Monitor for Trousseau's and Chvostek's. Check magnesium (↓Mg makes ↓Ca resistant to treatment). Post-thyroidectomy: keep calcium gluconate at bedside.

Hypercalcemia (Ca²+ >10.0 mg/dL)
Causes: hyperparathyroidism (most common), malignancy (bone metastases release calcium; some tumors secrete PTH-related peptide), immobility (bone breakdown releases calcium), excessive vitamin D or calcium supplements, thiazide diuretics (decrease calcium excretion)
Signs — "Everything slows down": muscle weakness, decreased DTRs, lethargy/confusion, constipation, nausea, polyuria (kidneys try to excrete excess → dehydration), kidney stones, shortened QT on EKG, cardiac arrest (severe)
Memory aid for symptoms: "Bones, stones, abdominal groans, and psychic moans" — bone pain, kidney stones, constipation/nausea, confusion/depression
Nursing Actions: hydration first (NS bolus to dilute and promote renal excretion), loop diuretics (furosemide — promotes calcium excretion), calcitonin (inhibits bone resorption), bisphosphonates (for malignancy-related), AVOID thiazide diuretics (retain calcium). Strain urine for stones. Ambulate if possible (weight-bearing keeps calcium in bones).

MAGNESIUM IMBALANCES

Hypomagnesemia (Mg²+ <1.6 mg/dL)
Causes: alcoholism (most common — poor intake + renal wasting), malnutrition, diarrhea, diuretics, proton pump inhibitors (long-term), DKA
Signs: similar to hypocalcemia (because ↓Mg → ↓Ca): tremors, hyperreflexia, tetany, seizures, Trousseau's/Chvostek's positive, torsades de pointes (prolonged QT), neuromuscular irritability
Nursing Actions: magnesium sulfate IV (infuse slowly — rapid infusion → flushing, hypotension, respiratory depression). Check DTRs before each dose (absent reflexes = toxicity). Keep calcium gluconate at bedside (antidote for MgSO₄ toxicity). Monitor respiratory rate (hold if <12). Dietary sources: green leafy vegetables, nuts, whole grains.

Hypermagnesemia (Mg²+ >2.6 mg/dL)
Causes: renal failure (can't excrete), excessive MgSO₄ administration (eclampsia treatment), excessive antacid/laxative use (Milk of Magnesia, magnesium citrate)
Signs — "Progressive CNS depression": lethargy, drowsiness → decreased DTRs (early warning) → respiratory depression → cardiac arrest. Think of it as progressive sedation.
Nursing Actions: STOP magnesium immediately. Calcium gluconate IV (antagonist). Monitor DTRs (absent = stop infusion), respiratory rate, cardiac rhythm. Dialysis for severe cases in renal failure. Avoid magnesium-containing medications in renal patients.

PHOSPHORUS IMBALANCES

Hypophosphatemia (PO₄ <2.7 mg/dL)
Causes: refeeding syndrome (insulin drives phosphorus into cells), chronic alcoholism, hyperparathyroidism (↑PTH → phosphorus wasting), vitamin D deficiency, prolonged antacid use (binds phosphorus in gut)
Signs: muscle weakness (including respiratory muscles → respiratory failure), confusion, seizures, bone pain, impaired WBC/platelet function
Nursing Actions: oral or IV phosphorus replacement. Monitor calcium (they're inverse). Monitor respiratory function. In refeeding syndrome: advance nutrition slowly, supplement phosphorus prophylactically.

Hyperphosphatemia (PO₄ >4.5 mg/dL)
Causes: renal failure (most common — kidneys can't excrete phosphorus), hypoparathyroidism, cell lysis (chemo, rhabdomyolysis), excessive intake (laxatives, enemas)
Signs: usually asymptomatic until calcium drops (inverse relationship) → then you see signs of hypocalcemia (tetany, Chvostek's, seizures). Chronic: calcium-phosphate deposits in soft tissues (calciphylaxis).
Nursing Actions: phosphate binders with meals (sevelamer, calcium acetate — they bind dietary phosphorus in the gut so it's excreted in stool). Restrict dietary phosphorus (dairy, nuts, processed foods, colas). Treat the hypocalcemia. Dialysis in renal failure.

CONNECT: → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 6: IV Therapy · → See Section 8: Renal Conditions, Cardiac Conditions

TEST TIP: NCLEX loves "which client should the nurse see first?" among electrolyte patients. Potassium and calcium are the most dangerous — cardiac arrhythmia risk. The patient with K+ of 6.8 gets seen before the patient with Na+ of 128. Peaked T waves on the monitor = see that patient NOW. Also: look for connections. A patient with persistent hypocalcemia who isn't responding to calcium replacement? Check the magnesium — you can't fix calcium without fixing magnesium first.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is reviewing lab results for four clients. Which client should the nurse assess first?

A. Client with sodium of 128 mEq/L
B. Client with potassium of 6.9 mEq/L
C. Client with calcium of 8.2 mg/dL
D. Client with magnesium of 1.4 mg/dL

Answer: B. K+ 6.9 is critically elevated — immediate risk of fatal cardiac arrhythmia (peaked T waves → widened QRS → cardiac arrest). This is a medical emergency requiring calcium gluconate for cardiac protection and interventions to lower potassium. The other values are abnormal but not immediately life-threatening.

CARDIAC CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 6: Cardiac Drugs · → See Section 7: EKG

Heart Failure (HF)

WHAT: The heart can't pump effectively enough to meet the body's metabolic demands. Can be left-sided, right-sided, or both (most common).

WHY IT MATTERS: Heart failure affects every other system — fluid backs up, organs get congested, and the body compensates in ways that ultimately make it worse. NCLEX tests your understanding of which symptoms belong to which side and what to do about them.

MECHANISM:
- Left-sided HF: Left ventricle fails → blood backs up into the lungs → pulmonary congestion
- Right-sided HF: Right ventricle fails → blood backs up into systemic venous system → peripheral edema, organ congestion
- Most common pattern: left failure → pulmonary HTN → right failure (so most patients have both)

RECOGNIZE:
Left-sided (pulmonary symptoms): dyspnea, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea (PND), crackles/rales, pink frothy sputum (pulmonary edema), cough, fatigue, tachycardia, S3 gallop ("Tennessee" — ta-KEN-ta)
Right-sided (systemic symptoms): jugular vein distension (JVD), peripheral edema (sacral if bedbound, pedal if ambulatory), hepatomegaly, ascites, weight gain, anorexia/nausea

ACT: Treatment focuses on reducing workload and volume
- Position: High Fowler's (reduces venous return and improves breathing)
- Monitor: daily weights (SAME time, SAME scale, SAME clothing — weight is the best indicator of fluid status; 1 kg = 1 liter of fluid)
- I&O strictly
- Sodium and fluid restriction
- Oxygen therapy
- Activity: balance rest and activity; avoid isometric exercises (Valsalva increases afterload)

MEDS:
- ACE inhibitors/ARBs: Purpose — reduce afterload and preload · Why? Vasodilation reduces the work the heart has to do
- Beta-blockers (carvedilol, metoprolol): Purpose — reduce HR and myocardial oxygen demand · Why? Started LOW and titrated slowly — reduces remodeling long-term
- Diuretics (furosemide/bumetanide): Purpose — reduce fluid volume · Why? Removes excess fluid → reduces preload → reduces pulmonary congestion. Monitor K+ (loop diuretics waste potassium)
- Digoxin: Purpose — increases contractility (positive inotrope) and slows HR · Why? Makes each heartbeat more efficient. Check pulse and K+ before giving.
- Aldosterone antagonists (spironolactone): Purpose — K-sparing diuretic, reduces aldosterone effects · Why? Reduces fibrosis/remodeling. Monitor K+ (hyperkalemia risk)

CONNECT: → See Section 6: Drug Classifications · → See Section 7: Hemodynamic Monitoring · → See Section 8: Pulmonary Edema

Myocardial Infarction (MI)

WHAT: Coronary artery occlusion → myocardial tissue death. Time = muscle. Every minute of occlusion destroys more heart tissue.

RECOGNIZE: Crushing substernal chest pain (may radiate to left arm, jaw, back, epigastric area), diaphoresis, nausea, dyspnea, anxiety/"sense of impending doom." Women and diabetics may have ATYPICAL presentations: fatigue, epigastric discomfort, jaw pain, dyspnea without chest pain.

EKG Changes (3 zones of injury):
- Ischemia: ST depression, T wave inversion (reversible with reperfusion)
- Injury: ST elevation (STEMI — actively dying tissue; needs immediate intervention)
- Infarction: pathological Q waves (dead tissue — irreversible)

ACT — Immediate Interventions (MONA is a memory tool, not a priority order):
1. Call for help / activate code
2. 12-lead EKG within 10 minutes
3. Aspirin 325 mg chewed (antiplatelet — unless contraindicated)
4. Oxygen if SpO₂ <94%
5. Nitroglycerin SL (up to 3 doses, 5 min apart; call 911 if no relief after first dose)
6. Morphine for pain (reduces anxiety and myocardial oxygen demand)
7. Prepare for reperfusion: PCI (percutaneous coronary intervention — balloon/stent, "door to balloon" target <90 min) or fibrinolytics if PCI unavailable (<12 hours from symptom onset)

Post-MI Nursing:
- Cardiac monitoring, serial troponins, serial EKGs
- Bedrest initially, progressive activity
- Cardiac rehab education
- Discharge meds typically: aspirin, beta-blocker, ACE inhibitor, statin ("ABCS")

CONNECT: → See Section 7: EKG · → See Section 6: Anticoagulants, Cardiac Drugs

PRACTICE:
A client arrives at the emergency department with crushing chest pain radiating to the left arm, diaphoresis, and nausea. After calling for help, what is the nurse's next priority action?

A. Administer morphine 4 mg IV
B. Obtain a 12-lead EKG
C. Start a nitroglycerin drip
D. Draw a troponin level

Answer: B. The 12-lead EKG within 10 minutes is critical — it determines whether this is a STEMI (needs emergent PCI) or NSTEMI (different management pathway). Everything else follows from this diagnostic decision. Aspirin can be given simultaneously, but the EKG guides treatment direction.

RESPIRATORY CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 7: ABG Analysis, Chest Tubes

COPD (Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease)

WHAT: Progressive, irreversible airflow limitation. Includes emphysema (destruction of alveoli → air trapping, ↓surface area for gas exchange) and chronic bronchitis (chronic productive cough, excess mucus production).

RECOGNIZE: Emphysema: barrel chest (hyperinflated lungs push ribs outward), dyspnea on exertion, pursed-lip breathing (instinctive — creates back-pressure to keep alveoli open), thin/wasted body habitus. Chronic bronchitis: productive cough >3 months/year for 2+ years, cyanosis, rhonchi. Both: wheezing, ↓breath sounds, prolonged expiration.

ACT:
- Oxygen: LOW flow (1-2 L/min via nasal cannula). Why? COPD patients retain CO₂ chronically — their respiratory drive has switched from CO₂-driven to hypoxic drive. High-flow O₂ eliminates the hypoxic drive → respiratory depression → CO₂ narcosis → death. Target SpO₂: 88-92%.
- Bronchodilators: albuterol (short-acting, rescue), ipratropium, tiotropium (long-acting maintenance)
- Corticosteroids: inhaled for maintenance, systemic for exacerbations
- Pursed-lip breathing: teach and reinforce (increases expiratory time → prevents air trapping)
- Nutrition: ↑calorie, ↑fat, ↓carb, small frequent meals. Why? Carb metabolism produces more CO₂ than fat metabolism — these patients already retain CO₂.
- Smoking cessation: THE most important intervention for disease progression

Asthma

WHAT: Reversible airway inflammation and bronchospasm triggered by allergens, exercise, cold air, infection, or stress.

RECOGNIZE: Expiratory wheezing (hallmark), dyspnea, chest tightness, cough (often nocturnal). Status asthmaticus: prolonged attack unresponsive to treatment — EMERGENCY. Danger sign: SILENT chest (no wheezing in a known asthmatic = complete obstruction = imminent respiratory arrest).

ACT: Position orthopneic. Rapid-acting bronchodilator first (albuterol via nebulizer or MDI). Systemic corticosteroids for acute exacerbation. Monitor peak flow. Teach trigger avoidance. Controller medications daily (inhaled corticosteroids) vs. rescue medications PRN (short-acting beta-agonists). If using rescue inhaler >2×/week, asthma is not well-controlled.

Pneumonia

WHAT: Lung infection causing inflammation and consolidation (fluid-filled alveoli can't exchange gas).

RECOGNIZE: Fever, productive cough (rust-colored sputum = classic bacterial pneumonia), pleuritic chest pain, tachypnea, crackles on auscultation, elevated WBC, consolidation on CXR.

ACT: Antibiotics (obtain sputum and blood cultures BEFORE starting antibiotics — but don't delay antibiotics to wait for culture results). Oxygen therapy. Fluids (loosen secretions). Incentive spirometry every 1-2 hours while awake. Positioning: good lung down (optimizes gas exchange). Pneumococcal and influenza vaccines for prevention.

Tuberculosis (TB)

WHAT: Mycobacterium tuberculosis infection. Airborne transmission. Major public health concern.

RECOGNIZE: Night sweats, low-grade fever, weight loss, fatigue, productive cough (potentially hemoptysis), positive TB skin test (≥5 mm for immunocompromised, ≥10 mm for high risk, ≥15 mm for low risk), positive QuantiFERON blood test, cavitary lesions on CXR.

ACT: Airborne precautions (negative-pressure room, N95 respirator for healthcare workers, surgical mask on patient during transport). RIPE therapy (→ See Section 6). Directly Observed Therapy (DOT) for compliance. Report to public health. Not contagious after 2-3 weeks of effective therapy with three consecutive negative sputum cultures.

Pulmonary Edema

WHAT: Fluid accumulation in the alveoli, most commonly from left-sided heart failure. Gas exchange becomes impossible when alveoli are flooded.

RECOGNIZE: Acute dyspnea, pink frothy sputum (pathognomonic), crackles throughout lung fields, tachycardia, anxiety, cyanosis, orthopnea.

ACT: This is an emergency.
1. Position: High Fowler's with legs dangling (reduces venous return)
2. Oxygen (high-flow, may need CPAP/BiPAP or intubation)
3. Furosemide IV (rapid diuresis — reduces fluid volume)
4. Morphine (reduces preload via venodilation + reduces anxiety/oxygen demand)
5. Nitroglycerin (reduces preload)
6. Monitor ABGs, SpO₂, I&O, respiratory status

CONNECT: → See Section 7: ABG, Chest Tubes · → See Section 6: Respiratory Drugs · → See Section 5: Positioning

TEST TIP: Two rules that NCLEX hammers relentlessly — COPD: the wrong answer is always high-flow oxygen. Asthma: the scariest patient is the silent one. No wheezing in an asthmatic doesn't mean they're getting better — it means complete obstruction. Silence is worse than noise.

PRACTICE:
The nurse is caring for four patients on a respiratory unit. Which patient requires immediate assessment?
A. A COPD patient on 2 L/min nasal cannula with SpO₂ of 90%
B. An asthma patient who was wheezing 30 minutes ago but now has a silent chest and is drowsy
C. A pneumonia patient with a temperature of 101.2°F and productive cough
D. A post-thoracentesis patient with slightly diminished breath sounds on the affected side

Answer: B. A silent chest in a known asthmatic is an ominous sign — it means air movement has stopped due to complete bronchospasm. Combined with drowsiness (CO₂ retention, respiratory fatigue), this patient is approaching respiratory arrest. Option A is at target for COPD. Option C has expected pneumonia findings. Option D has expected post-procedure findings. Why B works: Absence of an expected abnormal finding (wheezing) is more dangerous than the presence of it.

NEUROLOGICAL CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 7: Diagnostic Tests · → See Section 5: Positioning

Stroke/CVA

WHAT: Interruption of blood supply to the brain. Ischemic (clot — 87% of strokes) vs. hemorrhagic (bleed — 13%).

RECOGNIZE: sudden onset of: facial drooping, arm weakness, speech difficulty (use FAST: Face, Arms, Speech, Time). Ischemic: symptoms depend on vessel involved. Hemorrhagic: typically more severe onset, headache ("worst headache of my life"), vomiting, rapid deterioration.

ACT:
- Ischemic: tPA (alteplase) within 3-4.5 hours of symptom onset (must rule out hemorrhage with CT first). No anticoagulants/antiplatelets for 24 hours after tPA. Monitor for bleeding (the tPA breaks down ALL clots, not just the one causing the stroke). BP management per protocol.
- Hemorrhagic: NO tPA (would worsen bleeding). Manage blood pressure (↓BP to reduce further bleeding). May need surgical evacuation. Seizure precautions.
- Both: swallowing assessment before any oral intake (dysphagia → aspiration risk). Neuro checks every 1-2 hours. Positioning: ischemic = HOB flat (maximize perfusion to ischemic tissue); hemorrhagic = HOB elevated 30° (reduce ICP).
- Rehabilitation starts early: speech therapy, PT, OT

Increased Intracranial Pressure (ICP)

WHAT: Pressure inside the skull rises above 15 mmHg. The skull is a rigid container — any increase in brain tissue (edema), blood (hemorrhage), or CSF (hydrocephalus) increases pressure on the brain.

RECOGNIZE:
- Early: headache, restlessness, decreased LOC (most sensitive indicator), confusion, pupil changes (ipsilateral dilation), contralateral motor weakness
- Late (Cushing's triad — herniation is imminent): hypertension with widening pulse pressure, bradycardia, irregular respirations (Cheyne-Stokes or ataxic breathing). This is a neurosurgical emergency.
- Papilledema (optic disc swelling) on fundoscopy

ACT:
1. HOB 30°, head midline (promotes venous drainage)
2. Avoid: coughing, straining, Valsalva, suctioning >10 sec, clustering care activities, hip flexion
3. Maintain normothermia (fever increases metabolic demand → increases ICP)
4. Mannitol (osmotic diuretic — draws water from brain tissue). Monitor serum osmolality (hold if >320 mOsm/kg)
5. Hypertonic saline (3%) — same principle as mannitol
6. Controlled hyperventilation (short-term only — ↓CO₂ → cerebral vasoconstriction → ↓ICP). Target PaCO₂ 30-35 mmHg.
7. CSF drainage via ventriculostomy if present
8. Seizure prophylaxis (phenytoin or levetiracetam)

Seizures

WHAT: Abnormal, excessive electrical activity in the brain.

ACT — During a seizure:
1. Protect from injury (lower to floor if standing, clear objects away)
2. Do NOT restrain, do NOT put anything in the mouth
3. Turn to side (lateral position — prevents aspiration)
4. Time the seizure (>5 minutes = status epilepticus = emergency → benzodiazepine stat)
5. After: maintain airway, orient, assess, document
- Status epilepticus: lorazepam or diazepam IV first-line, then phenytoin/fosphenytoin for sustained control

Meningitis

WHAT: Inflammation of the meninges (brain/spinal cord coverings). Bacterial = life-threatening emergency. Viral = usually self-limiting.

RECOGNIZE: fever, severe headache, nuchal rigidity (neck stiffness), photophobia, positive Kernig's sign (pain with leg extension when hip is flexed), positive Brudzinski's sign (neck flexion causes involuntary hip/knee flexion), altered LOC, petechial rash (meningococcal — indicates DIC risk).

ACT: Droplet precautions (bacterial — until 24 hours after antibiotics). IV antibiotics immediately (don't delay for LP results). Lumbar puncture for diagnosis. Seizure precautions. Dim lights, quiet room. Monitor neuro status, ICP. Close contacts need prophylactic antibiotics (rifampin or ciprofloxacin).

Myasthenia Gravis vs. Guillain-Barré Syndrome

Feature Myasthenia Gravis Guillain-Barré Syndrome
Pattern DESCENDING weakness (starts face/eyes → body) ASCENDING weakness (starts feet/legs → body → respiratory)
Cause Autoimmune — antibodies attack acetylcholine receptors Post-infectious autoimmune — antibodies attack myelin sheath of peripheral nerves
Hallmark Ptosis (drooping eyelids), diplopia, weakness worsens with activity, improves with rest Progressive symmetric ascending paralysis, areflexia, potential respiratory failure
Respiratory risk Myasthenic crisis: severe weakness → respiratory failure. Trigger: infection, stress, missed medication Ascending paralysis can reach diaphragm → respiratory failure (peak at 2-4 weeks)
Treatment Anticholinesterases (pyridostigmine), immunosuppressants, thymectomy, plasmapheresis, IVIG Plasmapheresis, IVIG, supportive care (ventilatory support as needed), NO steroids
Crisis Myasthenic crisis (under-medicated) vs. cholinergic crisis (over-medicated). Edrophonium (Tensilon) test differentiates: improves myasthenic crisis, worsens cholinergic crisis Monitor respiratory function constantly — FVC <15-20 mL/kg or negative inspiratory force declining = intubate
Nursing priority Monitor swallowing (aspiration risk), respiratory function, medication timing Monitor ascending paralysis level, respiratory function every 2-4 hours, emotional support (sudden paralysis is terrifying)

Multiple Sclerosis (MS)

WHAT: Autoimmune demyelination of the CNS. Relapsing-remitting pattern most common.

RECOGNIZE: Charcot's triad: nystagmus, intention tremor, scanning speech (staccato). Plus: optic neuritis, paresthesias, spasticity, fatigue (often the most disabling symptom), bladder dysfunction, heat intolerance (hot bath → symptoms worsen).

ACT: Avoid heat exposure (worsens symptoms). Disease-modifying therapies (interferon, glatiramer, natalizumab). Corticosteroids for acute relapses. PT/OT for function. Bladder management. Energy conservation techniques. Emotional support (chronic progressive disease in young adults).

TEST TIP: Stroke: CT first — always rule out the bleed before anything else — then tPA if ischemic and within the window. Increased ICP: HOB 30°, head midline, and avoid anything that spikes intrathoracic or intra-abdominal pressure (Valsalva, coughing, straining, hip flexion). Cushing's triad? That's a LATE sign — if you see it, herniation is already knocking on the door.

PRACTICE:
A patient arrives in the ED with left-sided weakness, slurred speech, and facial drooping that began 90 minutes ago. CT scan shows no hemorrhage. The nurse anticipates which priority intervention?
A. Administer aspirin 325 mg and monitor neurological status
B. Prepare for tPA administration within the treatment window
C. Position the patient with HOB elevated at 30 degrees and dim the lights
D. Obtain a neurology consult for surgical evaluation

Answer: B. CT negative for hemorrhage + symptom onset within 3-4.5 hours = candidate for tPA (alteplase), the gold standard for acute ischemic stroke. Time is brain tissue — every minute of delay means more neurons lost. Aspirin is appropriate but not until 24 hours after tPA. HOB elevation is for hemorrhagic stroke/increased ICP, not acute ischemic (keep flat to maximize perfusion). Surgery is for hemorrhagic strokes. Why B works: Ischemic stroke within the window + no contraindications = tPA is the single most impactful intervention.

ENDOCRINE CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 6: Insulin, Thyroid Drugs · → See Section 5: Nutrition

Diabetes — DKA vs. HHS

Feature DKA (Diabetic Ketoacidosis) HHS (Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State)
Type Usually Type 1 (no insulin at all) Usually Type 2 (some insulin present)
Glucose >250 mg/dL (often 300-800) >600 mg/dL (often >1000)
Ketones Present (body burns fat → ketoacids) Absent or minimal (enough insulin to prevent ketosis)
pH <7.30 (metabolic acidosis) Normal or mildly acidotic
Onset Rapid (hours) Gradual (days to weeks)
Key signs Kussmaul breathing (deep, rapid — lungs compensating for acidosis), acetone/fruity breath, abdominal pain, N/V, dehydration Severe dehydration, altered LOC → coma, extreme hyperglycemia, seizures
Treatment priority 1. Fluids (NS initially — severe dehydration) 2. Insulin drip (Regular insulin IV) 3. Potassium replacement (insulin drives K+ into cells — can cause fatal hypokalemia) 4. Monitor glucose hourly Same principles but: fluid deficit is even more severe (may need 6-10 L). Insulin doses may be lower. Greater mortality risk (up to 20%).

Critical DKA Management Points:
- Monitor K+ closely — potassium MUST be >3.3 before giving insulin (insulin shifts K+ into cells → if already low, cardiac arrest)
- When glucose reaches ~250, switch to D5NS (prevents hypoglycemia while continuing insulin to clear ketones)
- Continue insulin until anion gap closes (ketoacidosis resolved), not just until glucose normalizes
- Identify and treat the precipitating cause (infection is #1)

DI vs. SIADH

Feature Diabetes Insipidus (DI) SIADH
Problem ↓ADH (central) or kidneys don't respond to ADH (nephrogenic) ↑ADH (excess secretion)
Fluid balance Massive water loss (polyuria — up to 20 L/day) Water retention (oliguria)
Sodium ↑ (hypernatremia — water lost, sodium concentrated) ↓ (hyponatremia — water retained, sodium diluted)
Urine Dilute, large volume, low specific gravity (<1.005) Concentrated, low volume, high specific gravity (>1.030)
Serum osmolality ↑ (concentrated blood) ↓ (diluted blood)
Signs Intense thirst, dehydration, tachycardia, hypotension Confusion, headache, weight gain without edema, nausea, seizures
Treatment Replace fluids. Central DI: desmopressin (DDAVP — synthetic ADH). Nephrogenic: thiazide diuretics (paradoxically reduce urine output), NSAIDs Fluid restriction (primary). Hypertonic saline for severe. Treat underlying cause. Demeclocycline (blocks ADH at kidney).

Memory aid: DI = Dry Inside (losing water). SIADH = Swimming In ADH Holding water.

Addison's vs. Cushing's

Feature Addison's Disease Cushing's Syndrome
Problem ↓Cortisol, ↓Aldosterone (adrenal insufficiency) ↑Cortisol (excess — from steroids or tumor)
Key concept Everything is DOWN (except potassium) Everything is UP (except potassium)
Signs Hypotension, weight loss, hypoglycemia, bronze/darkened skin, hyperkalemia, hyponatremia, fatigue, weakness HTN, weight gain (truncal obesity), moon face, buffalo hump, hyperglycemia, hypokalemia, hypernatremia, thin skin, easy bruising, immunosuppression
Crisis Addisonian crisis: severe hypotension, vascular collapse → death. Triggered by stress, infection, abrupt steroid withdrawal Doesn't typically have acute crisis (but chronic complications: osteoporosis, DM, infections)
Treatment Hormone replacement (hydrocortisone, fludrocortisone), ↑Na diet, ↓K diet, stress dosing (increase steroids during illness/stress) Treat underlying cause. If from exogenous steroids: taper slowly (NEVER stop abruptly — adrenal crisis). Surgical removal of tumor if applicable.
Nursing priority Teach never to abruptly stop steroids, wear medical alert, stress dose protocols Monitor glucose, infection signs (immunosuppressed — may not mount a fever), skin integrity, bone density

Thyroid Disorders

Feature Hypothyroidism Hyperthyroidism
Concept "Everything slows down" "Everything speeds up"
Signs Weight gain, cold intolerance, fatigue, constipation, bradycardia, dry skin, brittle hair, periorbital edema, depression, ↓reflexes Weight loss, heat intolerance, diarrhea, tachycardia/palpitations, tremors, insomnia, exophthalmos (Graves'), anxiety, ↑reflexes
Labs ↑TSH, ↓T3/T4 ↓TSH, ↑T3/T4
Treatment Levothyroxine (synthetic T4) — take on empty stomach, same time daily, takes 4-6 weeks for full effect Methimazole or PTU (block thyroid hormone synthesis), radioactive iodine (destroys thyroid tissue), beta-blockers (symptom control), thyroidectomy
Emergency Myxedema coma (severe hypothyroidism): hypothermia, bradycardia, altered LOC, respiratory failure → IV levothyroxine, supportive care Thyroid storm: extreme hyperthyroidism → fever >104°F, tachycardia, delirium, vascular collapse. Tx: PTU, beta-blockers, cooling, IV fluids, hydrocortisone

Post-Thyroidectomy Nursing:
- Monitor for hemorrhage (check for swelling in back of neck — blood pools posteriorly)
- Monitor for airway compromise (tracheal edema or hematoma compression)
- Keep tracheostomy tray and calcium gluconate at bedside
- Monitor calcium (parathyroids may be accidentally removed → hypocalcemia → tetany/laryngospasm)
- Semi-Fowler's, support head/neck
- Monitor voice quality (recurrent laryngeal nerve damage → hoarseness)

TEST TIP: DKA vs. HHS — know the split cold. DKA = Type 1, rapid onset, ketones present, Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath. HHS = Type 2, gradual, NO ketones, higher glucose, worse dehydration. The management for both starts the same way: fluids first, insulin second — and ALWAYS check K+ before you push insulin. Always.

PRACTICE:
A patient with Type 1 diabetes is admitted with blood glucose 580 mg/dL, rapid deep respirations, fruity breath odor, and K+ of 3.1 mEq/L. The provider orders an insulin drip. What should the nurse do?
A. Start the insulin drip as ordered and monitor glucose hourly
B. Hold the insulin, notify the provider, and request potassium replacement first
C. Start the insulin drip at half the ordered rate and recheck K+ in one hour
D. Administer the insulin drip and give oral potassium supplements simultaneously

Answer: B. Potassium MUST be ≥3.3 mEq/L before administering insulin. Insulin drives K+ into cells — in a patient who is already hypokalemic, insulin will push potassium further down, potentially causing fatal cardiac dysrhythmias. Replace potassium first, then start insulin. Why B works: This is a hard stop in DKA management — you do not give insulin to a hypokalemic patient regardless of how high the glucose is.

RENAL CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 7: Lab Values · → See Section 5: Nutrition

Acute Kidney Injury (AKI) vs. Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD)

Feature AKI CKD
Onset Sudden (hours to days) Gradual (months to years)
Reversibility Often reversible if caught early Irreversible, progressive
Causes Prerenal (hypovolemia, shock — decreased perfusion), Intrarenal (ATN, glomerulonephritis — damage to kidney tissue), Postrenal (obstruction — stones, tumor, BPH) Diabetes (#1), HTN (#2), glomerulonephritis, polycystic kidney disease
Key labs Rising BUN/creatinine, ↓urine output (<400 mL/day = oliguria), hyperkalemia Elevated BUN/creatinine, ↓GFR, hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, hypocalcemia, metabolic acidosis, anemia (↓erythropoietin)

Nursing Management (both):
- Strict I&O, daily weights, fluid restriction as ordered
- Monitor electrolytes (especially K+ — hyperkalemia is the most dangerous complication)
- Diet: ↓protein (pre-dialysis CKD — less waste for kidneys to clear), ↓Na, ↓K, ↓phosphorus, fluid restriction
- On dialysis: ↑protein (dialysis removes protein)
- Monitor for uremia (elevated BUN → nausea, metallic taste, confusion, pericarditis, uremic frost on skin)
- Medications: phosphate binders, erythropoietin (EPO) for anemia, vitamin D supplements, antihypertensives
- Dialysis access care: for AV fistula — feel for thrill (vibration) and listen for bruit (rushing sound). NO blood pressures, NO venipunctures, NO tight clothing on that arm. Alert: absent thrill/bruit = clotted fistula = emergency.

TEST TIP: AV fistula: feel for thrill, listen for bruit. If they're gone — it's clotted, and that's an emergency. NO BP, NO blood draws, NO tight sleeves on that arm, ever. Peritoneal dialysis: cloudy effluent = peritonitis until proven otherwise. Hemodialysis: weigh before and after — weight loss tells you how much fluid came off.

PRACTICE:
A nurse is assessing a patient with an AV fistula in the left arm who is scheduled for hemodialysis. Which finding requires immediate notification of the health care provider?
A. A palpable thrill over the fistula site
B. A bruit auscultated over the fistula site
C. Absence of thrill and bruit over the fistula site
D. The patient reports a tingling sensation in the left hand during assessment

Answer: C. Absent thrill and bruit indicate the fistula has clotted — this is a vascular emergency. The fistula is the patient's lifeline for dialysis. A palpable thrill and audible bruit are NORMAL findings indicating patency. Tingling warrants monitoring but is not immediately life-threatening. Why C works: The expected findings are PRESENT thrill and bruit. Their absence is the emergency.

GI CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 5: Nutrition, Elimination

Cirrhosis

WHAT: Irreversible scarring of the liver → portal hypertension → systemic complications.

RECOGNIZE: Jaundice, ascites, spider angiomas, palmar erythema, hepatomegaly → later liver shrinkage, esophageal varices (life-threatening bleeding risk), hepatic encephalopathy (confusion → coma from ammonia buildup), coagulopathy (liver makes clotting factors), caput medusae.

ACT: Lactulose for encephalopathy (converts ammonia to ammonium in the gut → excreted in stool; target 2-3 soft stools/day). Neomycin/rifaximin (reduces ammonia-producing bacteria). ↓Na diet. ↓Protein if encephalopathic. Paracentesis for tense ascites. Avoid hepatotoxic drugs. Bleeding precautions. Monitor for esophageal variceal bleeding (life-threatening — balloon tamponade, octreotide, banding/sclerotherapy).

Pancreatitis

WHAT: Autodigestion of the pancreas by its own enzymes.

RECOGNIZE: Severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, worse after eating, guarding, ↑amylase and lipase (lipase is more specific), Cullen's sign (periumbilical ecchymosis), Grey Turner's sign (flank ecchymosis) — both indicate retroperitoneal hemorrhage and poor prognosis.

ACT: NPO (bowel rest — stop stimulating the pancreas), NG tube if vomiting, pain management (morphine or hydromorphone), IV fluids aggressively, monitor for shock, position side-lying with knees flexed (reduces tension on abdomen). Resume low-fat diet when pain resolves and amylase normalizes. Avoid alcohol permanently.

GI Bleeding

Upper GI: coffee-ground emesis (old blood) or hematemesis (fresh blood), melena (black tarry stool — blood digested by stomach acid). Causes: peptic ulcer, esophageal varices, gastritis.
Lower GI: hematochezia (bright red blood per rectum). Causes: diverticulosis (most common), hemorrhoids, colorectal cancer, inflammatory bowel disease.

ACT: Assess hemodynamic stability first (vital signs, orthostatic changes). Two large-bore IVs. Type and crossmatch. Volume resuscitation (NS, LR, blood products). NG lavage may be ordered (checks for active upper GI bleed). Endoscopy for diagnosis and treatment. NPO. Monitor H&H (may take 4-6 hours to drop after acute bleed).

TEST TIP: Upper GI bleed gives you coffee-ground emesis or melena — black tarry stool, because stomach acid digested the blood. Lower GI bleed gives you bright red blood per rectum. Either way, the first move is the same: assess hemodynamic stability, then two large-bore IVs, type and crossmatch, volume resuscitation. Stabilize first, locate the bleed second.

PRACTICE:
A patient with a history of peptic ulcer disease reports sudden, severe epigastric pain. Assessment reveals a rigid, boardlike abdomen with absent bowel sounds. Vital signs: BP 88/52, HR 128, RR 26. What does the nurse suspect and what is the priority action?
A. GI hemorrhage — prepare for blood transfusion
B. Perforated ulcer — notify the provider and prepare for emergency surgery
C. Bowel obstruction — insert a nasogastric tube
D. Pancreatitis — administer IV pain medication

Answer: B. Rigid boardlike abdomen + absent bowel sounds + hypotension/tachycardia in a patient with PUD history = perforation with peritonitis until proven otherwise. This is a surgical emergency. GI hemorrhage presents with hematemesis or melena, not a rigid abdomen. Bowel obstruction has high-pitched (not absent) bowel sounds initially. Pancreatitis presents with epigastric pain radiating to the back, not a rigid abdomen. Why B works: Rigid abdomen = peritoneal signs = surgical emergency.

MUSCULOSKELETAL CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 5: Mobility/Positioning

Compartment Syndrome

WHAT: Increased pressure within a closed muscle compartment → compromised blood flow → tissue ischemia → necrosis. Most common after fractures, crush injuries, tight casts/dressings, or burns.

RECOGNIZE — The 5 P's (but don't wait for all five — pain is earliest and most reliable):
1. Pain out of proportion to injury (worst sign — especially pain with PASSIVE stretch)
2. Pressure (tense, swollen compartment)
3. Paresthesia (numbness/tingling)
4. Pallor (pale, cool skin)
5. Pulselessness (LATE sign — by this point, tissue damage may be irreversible)

ACT:
- This is a SURGICAL EMERGENCY — fasciotomy (surgical opening of the compartment to release pressure)
- Remove constrictive dressings, bivalve the cast
- Do NOT elevate the extremity above heart level (reduces arterial perfusion to already compromised tissue — keep at heart level)
- Do NOT apply ice (vasoconstriction worsens ischemia)
- Monitor neurovascular status every 1-2 hours on any casted/post-surgical extremity
- Compartment pressure >30 mmHg = surgical intervention needed

Total Hip Replacement

Post-op Precautions — Prevent Dislocation:
- Use abduction pillow between legs at all times
- Do NOT flex hip >90°
- Do NOT cross legs
- Do NOT internally rotate the affected leg
- Elevated toilet seat, reacher for putting on shoes/socks
- Signs of dislocation: sudden severe pain, affected leg appears shorter and externally rotated, inability to move
- DVT prophylaxis (anticoagulants, compression devices, early ambulation)

Fractures

Nursing Priorities:
- Neurovascular checks distal to injury (pulses, sensation, movement, color, temperature, capillary refill) — "CMS checks" (Circulation, Movement, Sensation)
- Cast care: do NOT stick anything inside (skin breakdown, infection). Elevate on pillows while drying. Petal rough edges. Report: hot spots (may indicate infection underneath), foul odor, drainage through cast, increasing pain, numbness
- Traction: maintain alignment, weights hang freely (never resting on bed or floor), do NOT remove weights. Assess pin sites for infection (redness, drainage, odor). Maintain countertraction (body weight).
- Fat embolism (long bone fractures): onset 24-72 hours post-fracture. Signs: sudden dyspnea, tachycardia, confusion, petechial rash (chest, axillae, neck — pathognomonic). Treatment: supportive (O₂, fluids). This is a medical emergency with high mortality.

TEST TIP: Compartment syndrome = the 6 P's: Pain (out of proportion, on passive stretch), Pressure (tense compartment), Paresthesia, Paralysis, Pallor, Pulselessness. Pain on passive motion is the EARLIEST reliable sign. Don't wait for pulselessness — by then, damage is done.

PRACTICE:
A patient with a tibial fracture in a long leg cast reports increasing pain rated 9/10 that is unrelieved by the prescribed opioid given 20 minutes ago. The nurse passively dorsiflexes the patient's foot, and the patient screams in pain. What is the nurse's priority action?
A. Administer an additional dose of pain medication
B. Elevate the leg above heart level to reduce swelling
C. Notify the health care provider immediately
D. Apply ice to the cast to reduce inflammation

Answer: C. Pain out of proportion to injury + unrelieved by opioids + severe pain on passive stretch = compartment syndrome until proven otherwise. This is a limb-threatening emergency requiring immediate fasciotomy. More pain medication masks the diagnostic sign. Elevation ABOVE heart level reduces arterial inflow and worsens ischemia (elevate TO heart level, not above). Ice doesn't penetrate a cast. Why C works: Pain on passive stretch is the earliest and most reliable sign of compartment syndrome — you don't wait for the other P's.

BURNS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 5: Nutrition · → See Section 6: IV Therapy

WHAT: Thermal, chemical, electrical, or radiation injury to the skin and potentially deeper tissues.

MECHANISM — Fluid Shifts:
- First 24-48 hours: massive fluid shift FROM intravascular TO interstitial space (third-spacing). Risk: hypovolemic shock
- After 48-72 hours: fluid begins to shift BACK into the vascular space. Risk: fluid overload, pulmonary edema

ACT — Fluid Resuscitation (Parkland Formula):
Total fluid in 24 hours = 4 mL × body weight (kg) × %TBSA burned
- First 8 hours: give ½ of total (time starts from TIME OF BURN, not arrival to hospital)
- Next 8 hours: give ¼
- Last 8 hours: give ¼
- Use Lactated Ringer's (isotonic, closest to plasma)

Rule of 9s (Adult %TBSA):
Head: 9% | Each arm: 9% | Anterior trunk: 18% | Posterior trunk: 18% | Each leg: 18% | Perineum: 1%

Burn Depth:

Depth Appearance Pain Healing
Superficial (1st degree) Red, dry, no blisters (sunburn) Painful 3-5 days, no scarring
Partial thickness (2nd degree) Blisters, moist, red/pink, edematous Very painful 2-4 weeks, minimal scarring if superficial
Full thickness (3rd degree) White, brown, or black; leathery (eschar); dry Painless (nerve endings destroyed) Requires grafting
4th degree Extends to muscle, bone, tendon Painless Requires extensive surgery, may need amputation

Nursing Priorities:
- Airway first (inhalation injury = #1 cause of burn death). Signs: singed nasal hairs, soot in mouth/nose, hoarseness, stridor → INTUBATE early (edema will worsen)
- Fluid resuscitation per Parkland
- Wound care: debridement, topical antimicrobials (silver sulfadiazine — check sulfa allergy), sterile technique
- Pain management (burns are excruciatingly painful — adequate analgesia, especially before dressing changes)
- Nutrition: ↑protein, ↑calories, vitamin C (massive metabolic demands)
- Monitor for infection (leading cause of death after initial resuscitation)
- Positioning: prevent contractures (splinting, ROM exercises, positioning opposite to contracture tendency)
- Emotional/psychological support

TEST TIP: Airway first, always. A burn patient with singed nasal hairs, hoarseness, or soot in the mouth/nose needs intubation NOW — don't wait for edema to close the airway. For Parkland: calculate from time of burn, not time of arrival.

PRACTICE:
A patient is brought to the ED after a house fire. He has burns to his face, chest, and both arms. His voice is hoarse, and you note singed eyebrows and soot around his nares. SpO₂ is 96% on room air. What is the nurse's FIRST priority?
A. Calculate total body surface area burned using the Rule of 9s
B. Establish two large-bore IVs and begin Parkland formula resuscitation
C. Anticipate endotracheal intubation and prepare equipment
D. Apply sterile dressings to all burn areas

Answer: C. Facial burns + hoarseness + singed eyebrows + soot in nares = inhalation injury. The airway will swell over the next hours and occlude. Intubate EARLY while you still can. The SpO₂ of 96% is falsely reassuring — carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin and pulse ox reads it as oxyhemoglobin. Fluid resuscitation and TBSA calculation matter, but a dead airway kills faster than hypovolemia. Why C works: ABC — airway always comes first, and these are textbook signs of impending airway loss.

SHOCK

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 6: IV Therapy · → See Section 7: Hemodynamic Monitoring

WHAT: Inadequate tissue perfusion → cellular hypoxia → organ failure → death if untreated. All types share the final common pathway of insufficient oxygen delivery to tissues.

Type Cause Key Signs CVP/PAWP Treatment
Hypovolemic Fluid/blood loss (hemorrhage, dehydration, burns) ↓BP, ↑HR, ↓urine output, cool/clammy skin, thirst ↓↓ Volume replacement (NS, LR, blood products). Stop the bleeding. Modified Trendelenburg.
Cardiogenic Pump failure (MI, HF, tamponade) ↓BP, ↑HR, pulmonary edema, JVD, crackles ↑↑ Improve cardiac output (inotropes: dobutamine). Reduce workload (vasodilators, diuretics). IABP. Do NOT give lots of fluid (heart can't handle it).
Distributive — Septic Systemic infection → vasodilation → maldistribution of blood flow Early (warm): fever, ↑HR, warm/flushed skin, bounding pulses. Late (cold): ↓BP, cool/mottled skin, altered LOC ↓ (relative — vessel bed expanded) Fluids aggressively (30 mL/kg bolus), broad-spectrum antibiotics within 1 hour, vasopressors (norepinephrine), source control, lactate monitoring
Distributive — Anaphylactic Severe allergic reaction → massive vasodilation + bronchospasm Urticaria, angioedema, stridor, wheezing, hypotension, tachycardia ↓ EPINEPHRINE IM (thigh) immediately — this is the #1 drug. Airway management. Fluids. Antihistamines (adjunct, not primary). Corticosteroids (prevent biphasic reaction).
Distributive — Neurogenic Spinal cord injury (T6 or above) → loss of sympathetic tone → vasodilation Hypotension + BRADYCARDIA (unique — other shocks have tachycardia), warm/dry skin below injury ↓ Fluids, vasopressors (phenylephrine), atropine for bradycardia. Keep warm.
Obstructive Physical obstruction of blood flow (tension pneumothorax, cardiac tamponade, massive PE) ↓BP, JVD, muffled heart sounds (tamponade), tracheal deviation (tension pneumo), sudden dyspnea (PE) ↑ Treat the cause: needle decompression (tension pneumo), pericardiocentesis (tamponade), anticoagulation/thrombolytics (PE). These don't respond to fluids alone.

General Shock Nursing:
- ABCs first (airway, breathing, circulation)
- Two large-bore IVs
- Continuous monitoring (cardiac, SpO₂, BP)
- Urine output monitoring (minimum 30 mL/hr — kidneys are the barometer of perfusion)
- Serial lactate levels (indicator of tissue hypoxia — normal <2 mmol/L; rising lactate = worsening shock)
- Vasopressors: run via central line when possible. Norepinephrine is first-line for septic shock. Dopamine for bradycardia-associated hypotension.

PRACTICE:
A client develops sudden facial swelling, throat tightness, wheezing, and a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg after receiving IV antibiotics. What is the nurse's priority action?

A. Stop the infusion and administer diphenhydramine IV
B. Stop the infusion and administer epinephrine IM
C. Maintain the infusion and administer oxygen
D. Stop the infusion and call the provider

Answer: B. This is anaphylaxis — a life-threatening distributive shock. Stop the causative agent AND give epinephrine IM immediately. Epinephrine is the ONLY drug that addresses all three problems simultaneously: bronchospasm (bronchodilation), vasodilation (vasoconstriction), and increased vascular permeability (stabilizes mast cells). Diphenhydramine is an adjunct, not a primary treatment. Calling the provider is important but epinephrine comes first — this is a standing protocol.

HEMATOLOGICAL CONDITIONS

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14% · Connects to: → See Section 6: Blood Products · → See Section 7: Lab Values

Sickle Cell Disease

WHAT: Genetic hemoglobin disorder (HbS) → RBCs sickle under low oxygen conditions → block blood vessels → tissue ischemia (vaso-occlusive crisis).

RECOGNIZE: Pain crisis (most common — severe pain in bones, chest, abdomen), fever, swollen joints, priapism. Triggered by: dehydration, infection, cold, stress, high altitude, hypoxemia.

ACT:
- Hydration (3-5 L/day — dilutes blood, reduces sickling)
- Pain management aggressively (these patients require opioids — do not under-treat or assume drug-seeking behavior)
- Oxygen (reduces sickling)
- Rest
- Avoid triggers (cold, dehydration, strenuous exercise, high altitude)
- Transfusions for severe anemia
- Hydroxyurea (increases fetal hemoglobin → reduces sickling episodes)
- Monitor for acute chest syndrome (fever + chest pain + new pulmonary infiltrate — leading cause of death in adults with SCD)

Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC)

WHAT: Paradox of simultaneous widespread clotting AND bleeding. Clotting factors and platelets are consumed forming microclots throughout the vasculature, leaving nothing available for normal hemostasis → uncontrolled bleeding.

RECOGNIZE: Bleeding from multiple sites simultaneously (IV sites, wounds, gums, mucous membranes, petechiae, purpura, hematuria, GI bleeding). Labs: ↓platelets, ↓fibrinogen, ↑PT/aPTT, ↑D-dimer, ↑FDPs.

ACT: TREAT THE UNDERLYING CAUSE (sepsis, obstetric complications, massive trauma, malignancy — DIC is always secondary to something else). Replace what's being consumed: FFP (clotting factors), platelets, cryoprecipitate (fibrinogen). Heparin may be used cautiously in some cases (controversial — stop the clotting cascade). Monitor: neuro status, renal function, skin integrity, all lines and puncture sites.

Anemia

RECOGNIZE: Fatigue, pallor, tachycardia, dyspnea on exertion, dizziness, ↓Hgb/Hct. Types: iron-deficiency (microcytic — ↓iron, ↓ferritin, ↑TIBC), B12/folate deficiency (megaloblastic/macrocytic), aplastic (pancytopenia — all cell lines ↓).

ACT: Treat the cause. Iron supplements (with vitamin C, on empty stomach, expect black stools). B12 injections (monthly for pernicious anemia — can't absorb oral B12). Folate supplements. Transfusions for severe cases. Energy conservation (pace activities, rest between tasks).

TEST TIP: Sickle cell crisis: hydrate, manage pain aggressively, give oxygen. And do NOT apply cold — cold causes more sickling, which is the opposite of what you want. DIC: the patient is bleeding AND clotting at the same time — treat the underlying cause, because the labs alone won't save them. Thrombocytopenia: bleeding precautions kick in at platelets <50,000; spontaneous bleeding becomes a real threat <20,000.

PRACTICE:
A patient with sickle cell disease is admitted in vaso-occlusive crisis with severe pain in the chest and extremities. Which nursing intervention is CONTRAINDICATED?
A. Administering IV morphine for pain management
B. Applying cold packs to the painful joints
C. Infusing IV normal saline at the prescribed rate
D. Administering supplemental oxygen via nasal cannula

Answer: B. Cold causes vasoconstriction, which worsens sickling by slowing blood flow and reducing oxygen delivery to tissues — exactly the opposite of what this patient needs. IV morphine is appropriate (sickle cell crisis is excruciatingly painful and undertreated pain is a documented problem in this population). IV fluids reduce blood viscosity and improve flow. Oxygen supports tissue oxygenation. Why B works: Cold = vasoconstriction = more sickling = more crisis. Apply warmth, never cold.

COMMON HALLMARK SIGNS — QUICK REFERENCE

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14%

Condition Hallmark Sign Why?
Pneumonia Rust-colored sputum Blood mixed with mucopurulent secretions
Asthma Expiratory wheezing Narrowed airways vibrate during forced expiration
Emphysema Barrel chest Chronic air trapping → hyperinflated lungs push ribs outward
Pulmonary edema Pink frothy sputum Plasma and RBCs leak into alveoli and get foamed by breathing
SLE (Lupus) Butterfly rash across cheeks/nose Immune complex deposition in skin
Appendicitis McBurney's point tenderness (RLQ), rebound tenderness, Rovsing's sign (LLQ palpation → RLQ pain) Inflamed appendix at McBurney's point; peritoneal irritation
Meningitis Kernig's sign, Brudzinski's sign, nuchal rigidity Inflamed meninges stretch with movement → pain
Pancreatitis Cullen's sign (periumbilical bruising), Grey Turner's sign (flank bruising) Retroperitoneal hemorrhage tracking through tissue planes
Cushing's syndrome Moon face, buffalo hump, truncal obesity Excess cortisol → altered fat distribution
Addison's disease Bronze/hyperpigmented skin ↓Cortisol → ↑ACTH → ACTH stimulates melanocytes
DVT Unilateral leg swelling, warmth, redness, calf tenderness Venous obstruction → fluid backup → inflammation
DKA Kussmaul breathing, acetone breath Lungs compensating for metabolic acidosis; ketone byproduct
Myasthenia gravis Ptosis, diplopia worsening with activity Antibodies block acetylcholine receptors → fatigable weakness
Guillain-Barré Ascending symmetric paralysis Autoimmune demyelination of peripheral nerves from distal → proximal
MS Charcot's triad (nystagmus, intention tremor, scanning speech) CNS demyelination in specific pathways
Pheochromocytoma Paroxysmal HTN with "5 P's" (Pressure, Pain-headache, Perspiration, Palpitations, Pallor) Catecholamine-secreting tumor
Preeclampsia HTN + proteinuria (after 20 weeks gestation) Endothelial dysfunction → vasospasm → end-organ damage
Autonomic dysreflexia Sudden severe HTN + pounding headache + bradycardia (in SCI above T6) Uninhibited sympathetic response below injury level
Pulmonary embolism Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, anxiety Clot blocks pulmonary vasculature → dead space ventilation
Cardiac tamponade Beck's triad: muffled heart sounds, JVD, hypotension Fluid in pericardium compresses heart → impaired filling
Tension pneumothorax Tracheal deviation (AWAY from affected side), absent breath sounds, JVD, hypotension Pressure builds → mediastinal shift → compresses contralateral lung and great vessels

BREATH SOUNDS REFERENCE

Physiological Adaptation · Exam Weight: 14%

Sound Character Cleared by Cough? Associated Conditions
Crackles (Rales) Fine, high-pitched popping/crackling; best heard on inspiration No Pneumonia, heart failure, pulmonary fibrosis, atelectasis
Rhonchi Low-pitched rumbling/snoring sounds Often yes Chronic bronchitis, pneumonia (large airway secretions)
Wheezes High-pitched musical/whistling; loudest on expiration Sometimes Asthma, COPD, anaphylaxis (bronchospasm)
Stridor High-pitched crowing sound; INSPIRATORY No Upper airway obstruction (croup, epiglottitis, laryngeal edema, foreign body). THIS IS AN EMERGENCY.
Pleural friction rub Grating/creaking; heard on both inspiration and expiration No Pleurisy, pulmonary embolism, pleural effusion
Absent/diminished Decreased or no air movement N/A Pneumothorax, pleural effusion, atelectasis, severe asthma (silent chest = critical)

SECTION 9: QUICK REFERENCE CARDS

These are your cheat sheets for the final stretch — everything distilled to its most testable form. Use them for rapid review, last-minute cramming, or when you need a concept refreshed in thirty seconds flat.

How to use these cards: These are your rapid-review reference pages. Not for learning — for confirming, reviewing, and cramming. Pull these out the night before your exam, during study breaks, and whenever you need a fast answer. Dense by design.

CARD 1: LAB VALUES

Complete Blood Count (CBC)

Test Normal Range Critical Values Clinical Significance
WBC 4,500–11,000/mm³ <2,000 or >30,000 ↑ Infection, leukemia, stress · ↓ Immunosuppression, bone marrow failure
Neutrophils 1,800–7,800/mm³ <1,000 neutropenia · <500 severe ↓ = initiate neutropenic precautions (no fresh flowers/fruit, private room, mask)
Hemoglobin F: 12–15 g/dL · M: 14–16.5 g/dL <7 consider transfusion ↓ Anemia, hemorrhage · ↑ Dehydration, polycythemia
Hematocrit F: 35–47% · M: 42–52% — Roughly 3× hemoglobin value · ↓ Fluid overload · ↑ Dehydration
Platelets 150,000–400,000/mm³ <50,000 bleeding risk · <20,000 spontaneous bleed ↓ Thrombocytopenia → bleeding precautions · ↑ Thrombocytosis → clot risk

Metabolic Panel

Test Normal Range Clinical Significance
BUN 5–20 mg/dL ↑ Dehydration, kidney disease, GI bleed, high protein diet
Creatinine 0.6–1.3 mg/dL ↑ Most specific for kidney function · Check before contrast dye
Glucose 70–110 mg/dL (fasting) <70 hypoglycemia → juice/glucose · >300 DKA/HHS risk
HgbA1c <5.9% normal <7% good DM control · >8% poor control → reassess regimen
Protein 6–8 g/dL ↓ Malnutrition, liver disease, burns
Albumin 3.4–5 g/dL ↓ Malnutrition, edema risk, drug binding changes

Liver Panel

Test Normal Range Clinical Significance
ALT 10–40 U/L Most specific for liver damage
AST 10–30 U/L Liver/cardiac damage (less specific than ALT)
Total Bilirubin <1.5 mg/dL ↑ Jaundice, liver/biliary disease, hemolysis

Coagulation

Test Normal Range Therapeutic Range Monitors
PT 9.5–11.8 sec — Warfarin effect
INR 0.8–1.2 (no therapy) 2–3 standard warfarin · 3–4.5 mechanical valve Warfarin — check regularly
aPTT 20–36 sec 46–70 sec on heparin Heparin — check q6h after dose change

Lipid Panel

Test Goal
Total Cholesterol <200 mg/dL
LDL ("bad") <100 mg/dL
HDL ("good") >60 mg/dL
Triglycerides <150 mg/dL

Therapeutic Drug Levels

Drug Therapeutic Range Toxicity Signs
Digoxin 0.8–2.0 ng/mL N/V, halos, bradycardia · Check K+ (hypokalemia ↑ toxicity)
Lithium 0.8–1.5 mEq/L >1.5 toxic · Tremors, confusion, seizures · Maintain Na+/fluid intake
Phenytoin 10–20 mcg/dL Nystagmus, ataxia, gingival hyperplasia
Theophylline 10–20 mcg/dL Tachycardia, seizures, N/V

ABGs (Normal)

Component Normal Memory Trick
pH 7.35–7.45 <7.35 = acidosis · >7.45 = alkalosis
PaCO₂ 35–45 mmHg Respiratory component (opposite of pH)
HCO₃⁻ 22–26 mEq/L Metabolic component (same direction as pH)
PaO₂ 80–100 mmHg Oxygenation — not acid-base
SaO₂ >95% <90% = hypoxemia

→ See ABG Interpretation Card (Card 3) for the full ROME method

CARD 2: ELECTROLYTE IMBALANCES

Memory aid: For most electrolytes, LOW = weakness/lethargy/hyporeflexia. HIGH = irritability/spasms/hyperreflexia. Potassium and calcium are the biggest cardiac risks.

Potassium (K⁺) — Normal: 3.5–5.0 mEq/L

Hypokalemia (<3.5) Hyperkalemia (>5.0)
Causes Diuretics (loop/thiazide), vomiting, NG suction, diarrhea, alkalosis Renal failure, K⁺-sparing diuretics, ACE inhibitors, tissue destruction, acidosis
Signs Muscle weakness, leg cramps, shallow respirations, ↓reflexes, constipation, weak/thready pulse Muscle twitching → weakness → flaccid paralysis, bradycardia, N/V, diarrhea, paresthesias
EKG Flattened/inverted T waves, U waves, ST depression Tall peaked T waves, widened QRS, absent P waves → V-fib/asystole
Treatment PO/IV KCl (NEVER IV push, NEVER >10 mEq/hr peripheral, always on pump, always diluted) Calcium gluconate (cardiac protection), insulin + glucose, kayexalate, dialysis

Sodium (Na⁺) — Normal: 135–145 mEq/L

Hyponatremia (<135) Hypernatremia (>145)
Causes SIADH, water intoxication, diuretics, heart failure, psychogenic polydipsia Dehydration, diabetes insipidus, excessive Na⁺ intake, Cushing's
Signs Confusion, headache, seizures, N/V, muscle cramps, cerebral edema Thirst, dry mucous membranes, restlessness, irritability, seizures, ↓LOC
Treatment Fluid restriction (if dilutional), hypertonic saline (3%) SLOWLY — risk of osmotic demyelination if corrected >12 mEq/24h Hypotonic fluids (0.45% NS), free water, correct slowly

Calcium (Ca²⁺) — Normal: 8.6–10.0 mg/dL

Hypocalcemia (<8.6) Hypercalcemia (>10.0)
Causes Hypoparathyroidism, vitamin D deficiency, pancreatitis, renal failure, blood transfusions (citrate binds Ca²⁺) Hyperparathyroidism, cancer (bone mets), immobility, thiazide diuretics
Signs Trousseau's sign (carpopedal spasm with BP cuff), Chvostek's sign (facial twitch on tap), numbness/tingling, tetany, seizures, laryngospasm "Stones, bones, groans, moans" — renal calculi, bone pain, constipation, confusion, muscle weakness
EKG Prolonged QT interval Shortened QT interval, widened T wave
Treatment IV calcium gluconate (SLOW — cardiac monitor), PO calcium + vitamin D NS hydration, loop diuretics (furosemide flushes Ca²⁺), calcitonin, bisphosphonates

Magnesium (Mg²⁺) — Normal: 1.6–2.6 mg/dL

Hypomagnesemia (<1.6) Hypermagnesemia (>2.6)
Causes Alcoholism, malnutrition, diuretics, diarrhea Renal failure, excessive Mg²⁺ intake (antacids, MgSO₄ overdose)
Signs Tremors, hyperreflexia, tetany, seizures (mimics hypocalcemia), torsades de pointes ↓Reflexes (FIRST sign), lethargy, respiratory depression, hypotension, cardiac arrest
Treatment IV/PO magnesium (slow IV, cardiac monitor) Stop Mg²⁺ source, IV calcium gluconate (antidote for MgSO₄), fluids, dialysis

Phosphorus (PO₄³⁻) — Normal: 2.7–4.5 mg/dL

Hypophosphatemia (<2.7) Hyperphosphatemia (>4.5)
Causes Refeeding syndrome, alcoholism, antacids (bind PO₄), DKA treatment Renal failure, hypoparathyroidism, chemo (tumor lysis)
Signs Muscle weakness, confusion, seizures, respiratory failure Usually asymptomatic; signs are due to reciprocal hypocalcemia (tetany, Chvostek's)
Key Relationship Inverse of calcium — when PO₄ goes up, Ca²⁺ goes down (and vice versa) Same inverse relationship

Chloride (Cl⁻) — Normal: 98–107 mEq/L

Hypochloremia (<98) Hyperchloremia (>107)
Causes Vomiting, NG suction (loss of HCl), metabolic alkalosis Dehydration, metabolic acidosis, excessive NS infusion
Signs Often tracks with sodium changes; metabolic alkalosis symptoms Often tracks with sodium changes; metabolic acidosis symptoms

CARD 3: ABG INTERPRETATION — ROME METHOD

Step-by-Step ABG Interpretation

Step 1: Look at the pH
- pH < 7.35 → Acidosis
- pH > 7.45 → Alkalosis
- pH 7.35–7.45 → Normal or compensated

Step 2: Identify the Primary Cause — Use ROME

Respiratory = Opposite: CO₂ moves opposite to pH
Metabolic = Equal: HCO₃ moves same direction as pH

If pH is... And CO₂ is abnormal in the opposite direction → Respiratory cause
If pH is... And HCO₃ is abnormal in the same direction → Metabolic cause

Step 3: Check for Compensation
- Uncompensated: Only one value (CO₂ OR HCO₃) is abnormal; pH is abnormal
- Partially compensated: Both CO₂ AND HCO₃ are abnormal; pH is still abnormal
- Fully compensated: Both CO₂ AND HCO₃ are abnormal; pH is normal (use pH to determine original problem: 7.35–7.39 = compensated acidosis, 7.41–7.45 = compensated alkalosis)

Common ABG Patterns

Condition pH CO₂ HCO₃ Example
Respiratory Acidosis ↓ ↑ Normal (or ↑ if compensating) COPD, sedation, pneumonia, airway obstruction
Respiratory Alkalosis ↑ ↓ Normal (or ↓ if compensating) Hyperventilation, anxiety, PE, fever, sepsis (early)
Metabolic Acidosis ↓ Normal (or ↓ if compensating) ↓ DKA, renal failure, diarrhea, lactic acidosis, aspirin OD
Metabolic Alkalosis ↑ Normal (or ↑ if compensating) ↑ Vomiting, NG suction, diuretics, excessive antacids

Worked Examples

Example 1: pH 7.28, CO₂ 58, HCO₃ 24
- pH < 7.35 → Acidosis
- CO₂ high (opposite to pH) → Respiratory cause
- HCO₃ normal → No compensation
- Answer: Uncompensated Respiratory Acidosis

Example 2: pH 7.50, CO₂ 30, HCO₃ 24
- pH > 7.45 → Alkalosis
- CO₂ low (opposite to pH) → Respiratory cause
- HCO₃ normal → No compensation
- Answer: Uncompensated Respiratory Alkalosis

Example 3: pH 7.33, CO₂ 50, HCO₃ 30
- pH < 7.35 → Acidosis
- CO₂ high (opposite to pH) → Respiratory cause
- HCO₃ also high (kidneys compensating) → Partial compensation
- Answer: Partially Compensated Respiratory Acidosis

Example 4: pH 7.30, CO₂ 38, HCO₃ 16
- pH < 7.35 → Acidosis
- CO₂ normal
- HCO₃ low (same direction as pH) → Metabolic cause
- Answer: Uncompensated Metabolic Acidosis (think DKA, renal failure)

CARD 4: ANTIDOTES

Toxin/Drug Antidote Key Nursing Points
Warfarin (Coumadin) Vitamin K (phytonadione) PO for non-emergent; IV SLOW for active bleeding. Monitor INR.
Heparin Protamine Sulfate 1 mg per 100 units heparin. Risk of hypotension, bradycardia, anaphylaxis.
Opioids (morphine, fentanyl) Naloxone (Narcan) Short half-life — may need repeat doses. Monitor for renarcotization. Can precipitate withdrawal.
Benzodiazepines (lorazepam, diazepam) Flumazenil (Romazicon) Risk of seizures (especially in chronic benzo users). Don't use if TCA co-ingestion.
Beta Blockers Glucagon Bypasses beta receptors. IV push then infusion.
Digoxin Digibind (digoxin immune fab) Check K⁺ before and after. Hypokalemia ↑ digoxin toxicity.
Methotrexate Leucovorin (folinic acid) "Leucovorin rescue" — give within 24–42 hrs of methotrexate.
TCAs (amitriptyline) Sodium Bicarbonate Widens QRS → give bicarb. Cardiac monitoring essential.
Acetaminophen (Tylenol) Acetylcysteine (Mucomyst) Most effective within 8 hrs of ingestion. Smells like rotten eggs — mix with cola. Check liver function.
MgSO₄ (magnesium sulfate) Calcium Gluconate Have at bedside for all MgSO₄ infusions (preeclampsia). Check reflexes, RR, UO.
Cholinergics Atropine Organophosphate poisoning: "SLUDGE" symptoms → atropine.
Anticholinergics Physostigmine "Hot as a hare, blind as a bat, dry as a bone, red as a beet, mad as a hatter"
CCBs (calcium channel blockers) Calcium + Insulin + Glucagon IV calcium first for cardiac protection.
Ethylene Glycol Fomepizole Antifreeze poisoning. Monitor renal function.
Methanol Ethanol (or fomepizole) Competitive inhibitor of alcohol dehydrogenase.
Insulin overdose Glucose / Glucagon D50 IV for conscious access; glucagon IM if no IV access.

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies for detailed drug profiles

CARD 5: POSITIONING

Neurological

Condition Position Rationale
↑ ICP / Head Injury HOB 30° midline, avoid neck flexion Promotes venous drainage from brain
Hemorrhagic Stroke HOB 30° Reduces ICP
Ischemic Stroke HOB flat or low Improves cerebral perfusion
Cerebral Aneurysm High Fowler's, dim/quiet Minimize stimulation, reduce rebleed risk
Post-LP Flat/supine 4–12 hrs Prevents spinal headache
Seizure (during) Side-lying, protect head Prevent aspiration, do NOT restrain
Post-supratentorial surgery HOB 30–45° Reduces ICP
Post-infratentorial surgery Flat, lateral Prevents brainstem herniation
Laminectomy Flat, log roll Maintain spinal alignment
Spinal cord injury Neutral, C-collar, log roll Immobilize until cleared
Autonomic dysreflexia Sit up (raise HOB) FIRST Lower BP immediately while finding cause

Cardiovascular

Condition Position Rationale
Post-cardiac cath Affected extremity extended, flat 4–6 hrs Prevent bleeding at access site
Shock Modified Trendelenburg (legs elevated 20°) Improve venous return

Respiratory

Condition Position Rationale
Asthma / COPD (acute) Orthopneic (tripod) Maximizes lung expansion
Post-bronchoscopy Flat, head hyperextended Maintain airway patency until gag reflex returns
Air / Pulmonary Embolism Left side, lower HOB (left Trendelenburg) Trap air in right ventricle
Thoracentesis (during) Sitting, leaning over bedside table Exposes posterior chest
Thoracentesis (after) Affected side UP Promotes lung re-expansion
Postural drainage Affected segment uppermost Gravity drains secretions

GI / Abdominal

Condition Position Rationale
Hiatal hernia Upright/high Fowler's Prevents reflux
Dumping syndrome Recline during meals; lie down 20–30 min after Slows gastric emptying
Enema Left Sims', knees flexed Follows anatomical curve of colon
Liver biopsy (after) RIGHT side × 3 hrs Applies pressure to puncture site via body weight
Paracentesis Flat or sitting Depends on procedure approach
Intestinal tubes Right side Facilitates passage through pylorus
NG tube (in place) HOB ≥ 30° Prevents aspiration
Hemorrhoidectomy Lateral Reduces pressure on surgical site

Musculoskeletal

Condition Position Rationale
Above-knee amputation Elevate 24 hrs → prone daily Prevent flexion contracture
Below-knee amputation Elevate foot 24 hrs → prone daily Prevent flexion contracture
Post-total hip replacement Don't flex >45–60°, abduction pillow Prevent dislocation
Buck's traction Elevate foot of bed Countertraction

Maternal / Pediatric

Condition Position Rationale
Prolapsed umbilical cord Knee-chest or Trendelenburg Relieve pressure on cord
Cleft lip (post-op) Back or infant seat, avoid prone Protect surgical site
Cleft palate (post-op) Prone Facilitates drainage
Spina bifida (pre-op) Prone Protect the sac

Other

Condition Position Rationale
Epistaxis Lean forward, pinch nares Prevent aspiration of blood
Post-thyroidectomy Semi-Fowler's, support head/neck Reduce edema, protect incision
Post-myringotomy Affected ear DOWN Facilitate drainage
Post-cataract surgery Unaffected side, night eye shield Prevent pressure on operative eye
Detached retina Position so detachment is dependent (gravity) Reattach retina by gravity
CBI (continuous bladder irrigation) Legs straight, flat Prevent catheter dislodgement
Tube feeding with ↓ LOC Right side, HOB elevated Facilitates gastric emptying, prevents aspiration
Peritoneal dialysis (poor outflow) Turn side to side Reposition catheter tip

CARD 6: IMMUNIZATION SCHEDULE

Childhood Immunizations

Age Vaccines
Birth HepB (dose 1), Vitamin K injection
2 months DTaP, IPV, HepB (dose 2), Hib, PCV13, Rotavirus
4 months DTaP, IPV, Hib, PCV13, Rotavirus
6 months DTaP, IPV, HepB (dose 3), Hib, PCV13, Rotavirus (if applicable), start annual Influenza
12–15 months Hib (booster), Varicella (dose 1), PCV13 (booster), MMR (dose 1), DTaP (dose 4), HepA (dose 1)
4–6 years IPV (booster), DTaP (dose 5), MMR (dose 2), Varicella (dose 2)
11–12 years Tdap (replace DTaP), HPV series, Meningococcal (MenACWY)

Adult & Elderly Immunizations

Population Vaccines
All adults Influenza — yearly · Tdap — once, then Td every 10 years
≥50 years Herpes Zoster (Shingrix) — 2 doses
≥65 years Pneumovax-23 (PPSV23) + PCV15 or PCV20 · Influenza — yearly

High-Yield Immunization Notes

  • Live vaccines (MMR, Varicella, Rotavirus, Intranasal Influenza): contraindicated in pregnancy and immunocompromised patients
  • MMR: Wait 28 days after live vaccine before becoming pregnant
  • Rotavirus: Oral vaccine, ages 2–6 months only, max age for last dose = 8 months
  • HepB: Can be given at birth — only vaccine safe on day 1
  • Egg allergy: Influenza vaccine is generally safe; observe 30 min. Severe anaphylaxis to eggs → give in supervised setting

→ See Section 3: Health Promotion & Maintenance for full immunization details

CARD 7: INSULIN

Types, Onset, Peak, Duration

Type Examples Onset Peak Duration Appearance
Rapid-acting Lispro (Humalog), Aspart (NovoLog), Glulisine (Apidra) <15 min 30–60 min 3 hrs Clear
Short-acting (Regular) Humulin R, Novolin R 1 hr 2 hrs 4 hrs Clear
Intermediate (NPH) Humulin N, Novolin N 4 hrs 8 hrs 12 hrs Cloudy
Long-acting Glargine (Lantus), Detemir (Levemir) Slow (1–2 hrs) No peak 24 hrs Clear

Critical Rules

  • Only Regular insulin can be given IV — all others are SubQ only
  • Draw up clear before cloudy (Regular before NPH) when mixing
  • Lantus (glargine): NEVER mix with any other insulin
  • Rotate injection sites — abdomen absorbs fastest → arms → thighs → buttocks
  • Hypoglycemia risk: Greatest at peak time. Teach patients to eat at peak times.
  • Before meals: Rapid-acting = give immediately before eating; Regular = give 30 min before
  • NPH + Regular: Most common prescribed combination. Can be mixed in same syringe.

Hypoglycemia vs. Hyperglycemia

Hypoglycemia (<70 mg/dL) Hyperglycemia (>300 mg/dL)
Onset Rapid (minutes) Gradual (hours to days)
Signs Cold, clammy, shaky, tachycardia, irritable, confused, hungry Hot, dry, flushed, Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath, polyuria, polydipsia
Treatment Conscious: 15g fast-acting carbs (4 oz juice, glucose tabs), recheck in 15 min · Unconscious: glucagon IM or D50 IV Fluids, regular insulin, electrolyte monitoring, treat cause
Memory "Cold and clammy, give some candy" "Hot and dry, sugar high"

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies and Section 8: Physiological Adaptation (DKA/HHS)

CARD 8: CONVERSIONS & VITAL SIGNS

Common Conversions

Conversion Value
1 tsp 5 mL
1 tbsp 15 mL
1 oz 30 mL
1 cup 8 oz = 240 mL
1 pint 2 cups = 16 oz = 480 mL
1 quart 2 pints = 32 oz = 960 mL
1 kg 2.2 lbs
1 grain 60 mg
°F → °C (°F − 32) ÷ 1.8
°C → °F (°C × 1.8) + 32
1 g water ≈ 1 mL (for diaper weights)

Vital Signs — Normal Adult

Parameter Normal Range Concern
Temperature 98.6°F ± 1° (37°C ± 0.5°) >100.4°F (38°C) = fever · >104°F (40°C) = hyperthermia emergency
Heart Rate 60–100 bpm <60 bradycardia · >100 tachycardia
Respiratory Rate 12–20/min <12 bradypnea (think opioids, ↑ICP) · >20 tachypnea
Blood Pressure <120/80 mmHg >130/80 Stage 1 HTN · >180/120 hypertensive crisis
O₂ Saturation 95–100% <94% supplement O₂ · <90% hypoxemia
MAP 70–105 mmHg Formula: (SBP + 2×DBP) ÷ 3 · <65 = organ hypoperfusion
CVP 2–8 mmHg ↑ Fluid overload, right HF · ↓ Hypovolemia

Vital Signs — Pediatric (Resting)

Age HR RR BP (systolic)
Newborn 120–160 30–60 60–80
Infant (1 yr) 100–150 24–40 80–100
Toddler (2 yr) 80–130 22–34 80–110
Preschool (4 yr) 80–120 20–30 80–110
School age (8 yr) 70–110 16–22 85–120
Adolescent 60–100 12–20 90–130

Vital Signs — Obstetric

Parameter Normal
Fetal Heart Rate 110–160 bpm
FHR < 110 Bradycardia — reposition, O₂, stop Pitocin, notify provider
FHR > 160 Tachycardia — assess for fever, dehydration, meds

CARD 9: INFECTION CONTROL

Standard Precautions (ALL patients, ALL the time)

  • Hand hygiene (soap & water or alcohol-based if hands not visibly soiled)
  • Gloves for body fluid contact
  • Gown if splash/spray expected
  • Mask + eye protection if splash/spray to face expected
  • Sharps disposal in puncture-resistant containers
  • Safe injection practices
  • Respiratory hygiene/cough etiquette

Transmission-Based Precautions

Type Airborne Droplet Contact
Room Private, negative pressure, door closed Private or cohort, door can be open Private or cohort
Mask N95 respirator (or PAPR) — fit tested Surgical mask within 3 feet (6 feet per some guidelines) Standard mask for splash risk only
Patient Transport Patient wears surgical mask Patient wears surgical mask Limit transport; cover wounds
Key Organisms Measles, Tuberculosis, Varicella (chickenpox), disseminated Zoster Strep (GAS), Pertussis, Influenza, Diphtheria, Meningococcal meningitis, Mumps, Rubella, Pneumonia (some), COVID-19 MRSA, VRE, C. diff, Scabies, RSV, Herpes zoster (localized), Impetigo, Lice, Rotavirus
Mnemonic "MTV" + Zoster (disseminated) "SPIDERMAN" Think: things that live on surfaces/skin

PPE Don/Doff Order

Donning (putting ON) Doffing (taking OFF)
1. Hand hygiene 1. Gloves (most contaminated — remove first)
2. Gown 2. Goggles/face shield
3. Mask/respirator 3. Gown (pull from shoulders, roll inside out)
4. Goggles/face shield 4. Hand hygiene
5. Gloves (last on) 5. Mask/respirator (remove by straps — don't touch front)
6. Hand hygiene (always last)

Memory: "Go go, go go" — Gown-Goggles-Gloves on; Gloves-Goggles-Gown off (simplified)

Sterile Technique Key Rules

  • 1-inch border of sterile field is contaminated
  • Below waist level = contaminated
  • Wet/damp = contaminated
  • If you turn your back on it = contaminated
  • Open sterile packages away from you (flap farthest from you opens first)
  • Pour solutions from 6 inches above the field
  • If in doubt, consider it contaminated

→ See Section 2: Safety & Infection Control for full details

CARD 10: DELEGATION

Scope of Practice

RN LPN/LVN UAP/CNA
Assessment (comprehensive & focused) Focused assessment on STABLE patients Vital signs on stable patients
Nursing diagnosis & care planning Reinforce (not initiate) teaching Bathing, grooming, dressing
Teaching (initial & complex) Administer PO, IM, SubQ meds Feeding (non-complex)
Evaluation of outcomes Sterile dressing changes Ambulation (stable patients)
IV push medications Monitor IV flow rate (NOT initiate) Intake & output measurement
Blood product administration Urinary catheterization (stable) Specimen collection
Unstable/complex patients Stable, predictable patients Routine, standard tasks
Triage & prioritization Trach suctioning (established) Turning & repositioning
Telephone orders from provider Report changes to RN Report observations to nurse

Five Rights of Delegation

  1. Right Task — Can this task be delegated? (Is it within their scope?)
  2. Right Circumstance — Is the patient stable enough? Is the situation predictable?
  3. Right Person — Does this person have the training and competency?
  4. Right Direction — Did you give clear, specific instructions?
  5. Right Supervision — Are you available for questions, and will you follow up?

Delegation Red Flags (NEVER Delegate)

  • Assessment, evaluation, or clinical judgment to non-RN
  • Unstable or unpredictable patient care to LPN or UAP
  • Patient teaching (initial) to LPN or UAP
  • Blood administration to LPN
  • IV push medications to LPN
  • Anything the person hasn't been trained to do

→ See Section 1: Management of Care for detailed delegation scenarios

CARD 11: COMMON SIGNS & SYMPTOMS

Condition Hallmark Sign/Symptom
Addison's disease Bronze skin, hypotension, hyperkalemia, salt craving
Cushing's syndrome Moon face, buffalo hump, truncal obesity, hyperglycemia, purple striae
Hypothyroidism Cold intolerance, weight gain, bradycardia, fatigue, constipation
Hyperthyroidism Heat intolerance, weight loss, tachycardia, exophthalmos, tremor
DKA Kussmaul breathing, fruity breath, dehydration, hyperglycemia >300
HHS Extreme hyperglycemia (>600), no ketones, severe dehydration, altered LOC
Heart failure (left) Dyspnea, crackles, orthopnea, pink frothy sputum, S3 gallop
Heart failure (right) JVD, peripheral edema, hepatomegaly, weight gain, ascites
MI Crushing chest pain radiating to jaw/left arm, diaphoresis, N/V
PE (pulmonary embolism) Sudden dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, anxiety
DVT Unilateral leg swelling, warmth, redness, positive Homans' (unreliable but tested)
Pneumothorax Sudden pleuritic chest pain, absent breath sounds on affected side, tracheal deviation (tension)
Pancreatitis Epigastric pain radiating to back, elevated amylase/lipase, Turner/Cullen sign
Appendicitis RLQ pain (McBurney's point), rebound tenderness, N/V, low-grade fever
Meningitis Nuchal rigidity, Kernig's sign, Brudzinski's sign, photophobia, fever
↑ ICP Cushing's triad (HTN, bradycardia, irregular respirations), altered LOC, projectile vomiting
Myasthenia gravis Progressive muscle weakness improving with rest, ptosis, diplopia
Multiple sclerosis Relapsing-remitting weakness, visual changes, intention tremor, Uhthoff's phenomenon (heat worsens)
Parkinson's disease Resting tremor ("pill rolling"), bradykinesia, rigidity, shuffling gait
Guillain-Barré Ascending paralysis (starts in legs), follows URI/GI infection
Pyloric stenosis Projectile vomiting (non-bilious), olive-shaped mass, visible peristalsis, hungry infant
Intussusception Currant jelly stools, sausage-shaped mass, colicky pain in infant
Epiglottitis Tripod position, drooling, dysphagia, "cherry red" epiglottis, NO tongue blade
Croup Barking (seal-like) cough, stridor, steeple sign on X-ray
Kawasaki disease Strawberry tongue, high fever >5 days, conjunctivitis, desquamation of hands/feet
Tetralogy of Fallot Tet spells (cyanosis), squatting position, boot-shaped heart on X-ray
Preeclampsia HTN (>140/90 after 20 wks), proteinuria, edema, headache, visual changes
Eclampsia Seizures in preeclamptic patient
Abruptio placentae Painful, dark red vaginal bleeding, rigid/board-like abdomen, fetal distress
Placenta previa Painless, bright red vaginal bleeding (no vaginal exam until ultrasound)
Pheochromocytoma Severe episodic HTN, headache, diaphoresis, tachycardia

→ See Sections 3–8 for full condition details

CARD 12: THERAPEUTIC DIETS

Condition Diet Key Points
Heart failure / HTN Low sodium (<2g/day) No canned soups, processed meats, chips; read all labels
