Renal failure (pre-dialysis) Low protein, low K⁺, low Na⁺, low PO₄, fluid restriction Limit bananas, oranges, tomatoes, potatoes
Renal failure (on dialysis) Higher protein, low K⁺, low PO₄, fluid restriction Protein needs increase with dialysis
Celiac disease Gluten-free No wheat, rye, barley, oats (unless certified); rice/corn OK
Crohn's / IBD (flare) Low residue/low fiber No raw fruits/vegetables, whole grains, seeds, nuts
Diverticulitis (acute) Clear liquids → low fiber High fiber only when NOT in acute phase
Diverticulosis (maintenance) High fiber Prevent flares with fiber + fluids
GERD / Hiatal hernia Small frequent meals, avoid spicy/acidic/fatty, no eating 3 hrs before bed Elevate HOB
Hepatic encephalopathy Low protein Reduces ammonia production
Cirrhosis Low sodium, possibly low protein Depends on ammonia levels
Dumping syndrome Low carb, high protein/fat, small frequent meals, no fluids with meals Lie down 20–30 min after eating
Pancreatitis (acute) NPO → clear liquids → low fat No alcohol ever
Cholecystitis Low fat Avoid fried foods, dairy fat
DM Type 1 Consistent carb counting, regular meals Match insulin to carbs
DM Type 2 Balanced, calorie-controlled, limit refined carbs Weight management key
Gout Low purine Avoid organ meats, shellfish, beer, red meat
Osteoporosis High calcium, vitamin D Dairy, fortified foods, weight-bearing exercise
Iron-deficiency anemia High iron + vitamin C (enhances absorption) Avoid calcium/antacids with iron (inhibit absorption)
PKU Low phenylalanine No aspartame (NutraSweet), limited protein
Nephrotic syndrome Low sodium, adequate protein Monitor albumin, manage edema
Burns (major) High calorie, high protein Metabolic demands massively increased
Post-tonsillectomy Clear cool liquids → soft foods NO red/brown fluids (mask bleeding), no straws, no crunchy foods
Cleft palate (infant) Breck feeder, slow flow, upright position Burp frequently, assess for aspiration
MAO inhibitor therapy No tyramine No aged cheese, wine, beer, smoked meats, soy sauce, avocados, bananas
Warfarin therapy Consistent vitamin K intake Don't eliminate green leafy vegetables — keep intake consistent
Potassium-wasting diuretics Increase K⁺ foods Bananas, oranges, potatoes, avocados, spinach

→ See Section 5: Basic Care & Comfort for nutrition details

CARD 13: DRUG SUFFIXES

Suffix Drug Class Key Nursing Implication
-olol Beta blockers (atenolol, metoprolol, propranolol) Monitor HR (hold if <60), BP, mask hypoglycemia signs in diabetics
-pril ACE inhibitors (lisinopril, enalapril, captopril) Dry cough (common SE), monitor K⁺ (↑), monitor renal function, angioedema risk
-sartan ARBs (losartan, valsartan, irbesartan) Similar to ACE inhibitors but NO cough. Monitor K⁺ and renal function
-dipine Calcium channel blockers — dihydropyridines (amlodipine, nifedipine) Peripheral vasodilation, edema, reflex tachycardia, avoid grapefruit
-statin HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors (atorvastatin, simvastatin) Take at bedtime (peak cholesterol synthesis at night), monitor LFTs, report muscle pain (rhabdomyolysis)
-prazole Proton pump inhibitors (omeprazole, pantoprazole) Take 30 min before meals, long-term use → ↓ Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺ absorption, fracture risk
-tidine H2 blockers (famotidine, ranitidine) Less potent than PPIs, fewer long-term risks
-azepam / -zolam Benzodiazepines (diazepam, lorazepam, alprazolam, midazolam) CNS depression, fall risk, respiratory depression, antidote = flumazenil
-triptan Serotonin receptor agonists (sumatriptan) For migraines, contraindicated with MAOIs, serotonin syndrome risk
-floxacin Fluoroquinolones (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin) Tendon rupture risk, photosensitivity, avoid in pregnancy/children, no antacids within 2 hrs
-mycin / -micin Aminoglycosides (gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin) Nephrotoxicity + ototoxicity, monitor trough levels, peak/trough timing
-cillin Penicillins (amoxicillin, ampicillin) Allergy/anaphylaxis risk, cross-reactivity with cephalosporins (slight)
-cephalosporin → -cef Cephalosporins (cefazolin, ceftriaxone, cephalexin) Ask about penicillin allergy, avoid alcohol (disulfiram reaction with some)
-vir Antivirals (acyclovir, valacyclovir, oseltamivir) Hydration important (renal clearance), start early for effectiveness
-azole Antifungals (fluconazole, ketoconazole) Hepatotoxicity, drug interactions (CYP450), monitor LFTs
-sone / -lone / -olone Corticosteroids (prednisone, prednisolone, methylprednisolone) Hyperglycemia, immunosuppression, don't stop abruptly (taper), Cushing's with long-term use
-lam / -pam Anxiolytics/Sedatives See benzodiazepines above
-pine Atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, clozapine) Metabolic syndrome, weight gain, clozapine → agranulocytosis (monitor WBC)
-done Opioids (methadone, hydrocodone, oxycodone) Respiratory depression, constipation, antidote = naloxone
-parin Anticoagulants — heparins (heparin, enoxaparin) Monitor aPTT (unfractionated), no aPTT needed for LMWH, antidote = protamine
-arin Anticoagulants — warfarin Monitor PT/INR, antidote = vitamin K, consistent vitamin K diet
-mab Monoclonal antibodies (rituximab, trastuzumab, infliximab) Infusion reactions, immunosuppression, biologic — store properly
-nib Kinase inhibitors (imatinib, erlotinib) Oral chemo agents, monitor CBC, LFTs
-ase Thrombolytics (alteplase, tenecteplase) Dissolve clots, major bleeding risk, strict time windows, neuro checks q15min

→ See Section 6: Pharmacological Therapies for complete drug profiles

CARD 14: CARDIAC RHYTHMS

Rhythm Identification Treatment
Normal Sinus Rhythm Regular, rate 60–100, P before every QRS None needed
Sinus Bradycardia Regular, rate <60, normal P waves Atropine if symptomatic; pacemaker if persistent
Sinus Tachycardia Regular, rate >100, normal P waves Treat underlying cause (pain, fever, hypovolemia, anxiety)
Atrial Fibrillation (A-fib) Irregularly irregular, no identifiable P waves, fibrillatory baseline Rate control (diltiazem, beta blockers), anticoagulation (stroke risk), cardioversion
Atrial Flutter Regular or irregular, "sawtooth" P waves, usually 300 atrial rate with block Rate control, cardioversion, ablation
SVT Very fast (150–250), regular, narrow QRS, P waves hidden Vagal maneuvers → adenosine (rapid IV push + flush) → cardioversion
1st Degree AV Block Regular, PR interval >0.20 sec, all P waves conducted Usually benign, monitor
2nd Degree Type I (Wenckebach) Progressive PR prolongation → dropped QRS → cycle repeats Usually benign, monitor. Atropine if symptomatic
2nd Degree Type II Constant PR interval with randomly dropped QRS More serious. Pacemaker likely needed. Atropine, transcutaneous pacing
3rd Degree (Complete) Heart Block P waves and QRS completely independent, regular but dissociated Pacemaker required. Transcutaneous pacing as bridge. Do NOT give atropine for wide QRS
PVC Wide, bizarre QRS without preceding P wave Occasional: monitor · Frequent/symptomatic: amiodarone, lidocaine · >6/min, couplets, R-on-T: danger
V-Tach (with pulse) Wide QRS, regular, rate 100–250, no P waves Amiodarone or lidocaine, synchronized cardioversion
V-Tach (pulseless) Same appearance, no pulse CPR + defibrillation (shockable rhythm), epinephrine, amiodarone
V-Fib Chaotic, no identifiable waves CPR + defibrillation (shockable rhythm), epinephrine, amiodarone
Asystole Flat line (confirm in 2 leads) CPR, epinephrine — NOT shockable. Confirm in 2 leads (rule out loose lead)
PEA Organized rhythm on monitor but NO pulse CPR, epinephrine — NOT shockable. Treat H's & T's
Torsades de Pointes Twisting QRS around baseline, polymorphic V-tach IV magnesium, overdrive pacing, defibrillation if pulseless

Key EKG Intervals

  • PR interval: 0.12–0.20 sec (3–5 small boxes)
  • QRS complex: 0.06–0.10 sec (<3 small boxes = narrow = supraventricular origin)
  • QT interval: <0.44 sec (prolonged QT → torsades risk)

→ See Section 8: Physiological Adaptation for full cardiac content

CARD 15: STAGES OF LABOR & FETAL MONITORING

Stages of Labor

Stage Phase Dilation Contractions Duration Nursing Care
1st Latent 0–6 cm Mild, q5–30 min, 30–45 sec Longest phase (varies widely) Encourage ambulation, hydration, comfort measures, may go home if early
1st Active 6–8 cm Moderate, q3–5 min, 40–60 sec Average 3–6 hrs (nullipara) Breathing techniques, positioning, pain management options, monitor FHR
1st Transition 8–10 cm Strong, q2–3 min, 60–90 sec 30 min – 2 hrs Most intense. N/V common. Provide constant support. "I can't do this" is normal.
2nd Pushing 10 cm (complete) Urge to push, q2–3 min Up to 2 hrs (nullipara) Coach pushing with contractions, monitor FHR with each push, position changes
3rd Placenta delivery — — 5–30 min Assess placenta for completeness (3 vessels: 2 arteries, 1 vein), fundal massage
4th Recovery — — 1–2 hrs postpartum VS q15min × 1hr, assess fundus (firm, midline, at/below umbilicus), lochia, bonding

VEAL CHOP — Fetal Heart Rate Patterns

FHR Pattern Cause Memory
Variable decelerations Cord compression Abrupt onset/offset, "V" shape. Reposition mother, check for prolapsed cord
Early decelerations Head compression Mirror contractions. Normal. No intervention needed
Accelerations OK (oxygenated) Reassuring sign. Rise ≥15 bpm × ≥15 sec
Late decelerations Placental insufficiency Begin after contraction peaks, slow return. CONCERNING → reposition left side, O₂, stop Pitocin, IV fluids, notify provider

Fetal Heart Rate Essentials

  • Normal FHR: 110–160 bpm
  • Moderate variability: Reassuring (fluctuation 6–25 bpm)
  • Absent/minimal variability: Concerning — may indicate fetal distress or sleep cycle
  • Prolonged deceleration: FHR <110 for >2 min. Emergency — prepare for delivery

Interventions for Non-Reassuring FHR

  1. Reposition to left lateral (improves placental perfusion)
  2. Administer O₂ via non-rebreather mask at 8–10 L/min
  3. Stop oxytocin (Pitocin) if running
  4. Increase IV fluids
  5. Notify provider
  6. Prepare for possible emergency cesarean delivery

→ See Section 3: Health Promotion & Maintenance for complete OB content

CARD 16: EMERGENCY REFERENCE

H's and T's (Reversible Causes of Cardiac Arrest)

H's T's
Hypovolemia Tension pneumothorax
Hypoxia Tamponade (cardiac)
Hydrogen ion (acidosis) Toxins (drug OD)
Hypo/Hyperkalemia Thrombosis (coronary — MI)
Hypothermia Thrombosis (pulmonary — PE)

Types of Shock

Type Cause Signs Priority Treatment
Hypovolemic Blood/fluid loss (hemorrhage, burns, dehydration) ↓ BP, ↑ HR, ↓ CVP, cool/pale/clammy, ↓ UO Volume replacement — NS, LR, blood products. Stop bleeding.
Cardiogenic Pump failure (MI, severe HF, tamponade) ↓ BP, ↑ HR, ↑ CVP/JVD, crackles, dyspnea Treat cause. Vasopressors, inotropes (dobutamine). IABP. Avoid fluid overload.
Distributive — Septic Widespread infection → vasodilation EARLY: warm, flushed (warm shock) · LATE: cool, mottled (cold shock) Sepsis bundle: cultures, antibiotics within 1 hr, 30 mL/kg crystalloid, vasopressors (norepinephrine), lactate monitoring
Distributive — Anaphylactic Severe allergic reaction → vasodilation + airway edema Urticaria, bronchospasm, angioedema, ↓ BP, stridor Epinephrine IM (first-line), airway management, IV fluids, diphenhydramine, steroids
Distributive — Neurogenic Loss of sympathetic tone (spinal cord injury) ↓ BP, ↓ HR (bradycardia — unique), warm/dry below level of injury IV fluids, vasopressors (phenylephrine), atropine for bradycardia
Obstructive Physical obstruction (PE, tension pneumo, tamponade) ↓ BP, ↑ HR, JVD, muffled heart sounds (tamponade) Treat obstruction: needle decompression (pneumo), pericardiocentesis (tamponade), thrombolytics (PE)

CPR Basics (Current AHA Guidelines)

Component Adult Child (1–puberty) Infant (<1 yr)
Compression depth ≥2 inches (5 cm) ~2 inches (5 cm) 1.5 inches (4 cm)
Compression rate 100–120/min 100–120/min 100–120/min
C:V ratio (1 rescuer) 30:2 30:2 30:2
C:V ratio (2 rescuers) 30:2 15:2 15:2
Hand placement 2 hands, lower half of sternum 1 or 2 hands, lower half of sternum 2 fingers or thumb-encircling technique
AED Use immediately Use pediatric pads/dose attenuator if available Use pediatric system if available

Rapid Response / Code Blue Triggers

Activate rapid response for: RR <10 or >30, HR <40 or >130, SBP <90, O₂ Sat <90% despite O₂, acute mental status change, new-onset chest pain, seizure, acute significant bleeding, staff "gut feeling" that something is wrong.

Emergency Medication Quick Reference

Medication Use Key Points
Epinephrine Cardiac arrest, anaphylaxis ACLS: 1 mg IV q3–5 min · Anaphylaxis: 0.3–0.5 mg IM (auto-injector)
Amiodarone V-fib, pulseless V-tach 300 mg IV push (first dose), 150 mg (second dose)
Atropine Symptomatic bradycardia 0.5–1 mg IV q3–5 min, max 3 mg
Adenosine SVT 6 mg rapid IV push + NS flush → 12 mg if needed. Use proximal IV site.
Naloxone Opioid overdose 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/IN, repeat q2–3 min. Short half-life — monitor for renarcotization
D50 (Dextrose 50%) Severe hypoglycemia 25–50 mL IV push. Recheck glucose q15 min
Glucagon Hypoglycemia (no IV access) 1 mg IM/SubQ. Position on side (causes N/V)

Fire Safety

RACE (in order):
- Rescue anyone in immediate danger
- Activate the alarm
- Contain the fire (close doors/windows)
- Extinguish or Evacuate

PASS (fire extinguisher):
- Pull the pin
- Aim at the base of the fire
- Squeeze the handle
- Sweep side to side

→ See Section 2: Safety & Infection Control and Section 8: Physiological Adaptation for complete emergency content

SECTION 10: PRACTICE SCENARIOS — NGN Clinical Judgment Format

Reading about clinical judgment is one thing — doing it is another. These scenarios put you in the NGN format so you can practice the thinking NCLEX actually tests: recognizing cues, generating hypotheses, prioritizing actions, and evaluating outcomes. Work them like real test questions — no peeking at rationales until you've committed to an answer.

How to use these scenarios: Work through each case as if you're at the bedside. Read the clinical information carefully, then answer the questions in order — they follow the NCJMM steps from Recognize Cues through Evaluate Outcomes. Cover the rationales until you've answered. These are designed to feel like the real NGN case studies on your exam.

SCENARIO 1: Post-Operative Complications (Med-Surg)

Clinical Setting

Medical-surgical unit, day shift.

Patient Information

Mr. James Whitfield, 58 years old. Status post right hemicolectomy (colon resection) 18 hours ago for colon cancer. History of type 2 diabetes, hypertension, and obesity (BMI 34). Current medications include metformin (held post-op), lisinopril 10 mg daily, enoxaparin 40 mg SubQ daily, morphine PCA, and IV 0.9% NS at 125 mL/hr.

Nurse's Notes — 0630

Patient resting in bed with eyes closed. Responds to name but appears drowsy. Abdominal dressing dry and intact with Jackson-Pratt drain in place — 50 mL serosanguineous drainage over last 8 hours. NG tube to low intermittent suction — 200 mL dark green drainage. Foley catheter draining clear amber urine. Patient denies pain, rates 2/10 "when I move." Has not ambulated since surgery. Sequential compression devices in place bilaterally.

Vital Signs — 0630

Parameter Value
Temperature 100.8°F (38.2°C)
Heart Rate 108 bpm
Blood Pressure 98/62 mmHg
Respiratory Rate 22/min
O₂ Saturation 93% on room air

Lab Results (drawn at 0600)

Test Result Reference
WBC 14,200/mm³ 4,500–11,000
Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL 14–16.5 (male)
Hematocrit 29% 42–52% (male)
Platelets 188,000/mm³ 150,000–400,000
Glucose 198 mg/dL 70–110
Potassium 3.2 mEq/L 3.5–5.0
BUN 28 mg/dL 5–20
Creatinine 1.1 mg/dL 0.6–1.3

Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

Instructions: Highlight the assessment findings in the nurse's notes and vital signs that require follow-up.

From the nurse's notes and vital signs, identify the findings that are of concern by selecting them below:

  • [ ] Patient responds to name but appears drowsy
  • [ ] Jackson-Pratt drain: 50 mL serosanguineous drainage over 8 hours
  • [ ] NG tube: 200 mL dark green drainage
  • [ ] Patient denies pain, rates 2/10
  • [ ] Has not ambulated since surgery
  • [ ] Temperature 100.8°F (38.2°C)
  • [ ] Heart rate 108 bpm
  • [ ] Blood pressure 98/62 mmHg
  • [ ] Respiratory rate 22/min
  • [ ] O₂ saturation 93% on room air
  • [ ] WBC 14,200/mm³
  • [ ] Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL
  • [ ] Glucose 198 mg/dL
  • [ ] Potassium 3.2 mEq/L
  • [ ] BUN 28 mg/dL

Correct Answers: Patient appears drowsy | Has not ambulated since surgery | Temperature 100.8°F | Heart rate 108 bpm | Blood pressure 98/62 mmHg | O₂ saturation 93% on room air | WBC 14,200 | Hemoglobin 9.8 g/dL | Glucose 198 mg/dL | Potassium 3.2 mEq/L | BUN 28 mg/dL

Rationale:
- Drowsiness at 18 hrs post-op warrants attention — could indicate overmedication (morphine PCA), hypotension effects, or early sepsis.
- Not ambulating by 18 hours post-op increases risk for DVT/PE, pneumonia, and ileus.
- Temp 100.8°F — Low-grade fever is expected in the first 24 hrs ("surgical stress response"), but combined with elevated WBC and tachycardia, this cluster raises concern.
- HR 108 — Tachycardia may indicate pain, hypovolemia, fever response, or early shock.
- BP 98/62 — Hypotension in a patient with baseline hypertension is significant. Could indicate hypovolemia or early sepsis.
- O₂ Sat 93% — Below normal (95–100%). Consider atelectasis (most common post-op pulmonary complication), PE, or fluid overload.
- WBC 14,200 — Elevated. Expected mild elevation post-op, but this combined with fever and tachycardia needs monitoring.
- Hgb 9.8 — Below normal for a male. Expected drop post-surgery, but monitor for bleeding.
- Glucose 198 — Elevated, likely due to surgical stress and held metformin. Needs insulin coverage.
- K⁺ 3.2 — Low. NG suction loses potassium. Risk for cardiac arrhythmias, ileus, and muscle weakness.
- BUN 28 — Mildly elevated. Consider dehydration or GI blood (swallowed blood from NG).

Why not concerning:
- JP drain output of 50 mL serosanguineous over 8 hours is normal post-abdominal surgery.
- NG drainage of 200 mL dark green is expected (bile and gastric contents).
- Pain rating of 2/10 is well-controlled, though in context of drowsiness, consider whether PCA overuse is contributing.
- Respiratory rate of 22 is mildly elevated but not critically abnormal — still warrants attention in context.
- Creatinine 1.1 is within normal limits.
- Platelets are normal.

Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Matrix Format)

Instructions: For each assessment finding cluster, indicate the most likely underlying cause.

Finding Cluster Hypovolemia Infection/Sepsis Opioid Oversedation Electrolyte Imbalance
Tachycardia + hypotension + low hemoglobin + elevated BUN X
Fever + elevated WBC + tachycardia X
Drowsiness + pain rating 2/10 + O₂ sat 93% X
Potassium 3.2 + NG suction + no ambulation X

Rationale:
- Hypovolemia cluster: Tachycardia and hypotension are classic compensatory signs. Low Hgb suggests either surgical blood loss or hemodilution. Elevated BUN with normal creatinine suggests dehydration (prerenal azotemia). This patient's baseline BP is likely higher given his HTN history, making 98/62 more significant.
- Infection/Sepsis cluster: Fever + WBC + tachycardia = the early sepsis triad. At 18 hours post-abdominal surgery, consider surgical site infection (early for this, but not impossible), urinary tract infection (Foley in place), or pneumonia (not ambulating, low O₂ sat).
- Opioid oversedation cluster: Drowsiness + comfortable pain level + decreased O₂ sat suggests PCA may be providing more than needed. Morphine causes respiratory depression and sedation.
- Electrolyte cluster: NG suction depletes potassium, sodium, and chloride. K⁺ 3.2 increases risk for arrhythmias and contributes to ileus (which delays recovery). Lack of ambulation worsens ileus risk.

Question 3 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Drag-and-Drop/Ranking Format)

Instructions: Rank the following concerns in order of priority (1 = address first, 4 = address last).

Rank these nursing concerns from highest to lowest priority:

Priority Concern
1 Hypotension and potential hypovolemia
2 Opioid oversedation with decreased oxygenation
3 Hypokalemia requiring replacement
4 Early ambulation for post-operative recovery

Rationale:
- Hypotension/hypovolemia is first because inadequate perfusion threatens organ function. BP 98/62 with tachycardia indicates the body is compensating — if this continues without intervention, the patient could progress to shock. ABCs: circulation is at risk.
- Opioid oversedation is second because O₂ sat 93% + drowsiness indicates respiratory compromise. ABCs: breathing/oxygenation. Assess respiratory rate and depth, consider holding PCA, apply supplemental O₂.
- Hypokalemia is third because K⁺ 3.2 needs correction to prevent cardiac arrhythmias, but it's not immediately life-threatening at this level.
- Ambulation is fourth because it's essential for recovery but is not the immediate priority when the patient is hemodynamically unstable.

Question 4 — Generate Solutions (SATA Format)

Instructions: Select all interventions that are appropriate for this patient at this time.

  • [ ] A. Increase IV fluid rate and assess fluid status
  • [ ] B. Hold the morphine PCA and reassess pain management
  • [ ] C. Apply supplemental oxygen via nasal cannula
  • [ ] D. Administer IV potassium chloride as ordered
  • [ ] E. Notify the healthcare provider of vital sign changes
  • [ ] F. Obtain a blood culture before starting antibiotics
  • [ ] G. Administer a bolus of D5W for glucose management
  • [ ] H. Encourage incentive spirometry every 1–2 hours
  • [ ] I. Check most recent PCA usage history
  • [ ] J. Assist patient to ambulate in the hallway immediately

Correct Answers: A, B, C, D, E, F, H, I

Rationale:
- (A) Correct — Fluid resuscitation addresses hypovolemia. Assess skin turgor, mucous membranes, urine output trend.
- (B) Correct — With drowsiness and O₂ sat 93%, the PCA should be held and pain reassessed. Consider non-opioid alternatives.
- (C) Correct — O₂ sat 93% warrants supplemental oxygen. Apply 2L NC, titrate to maintain >95%.
- (D) Correct — K⁺ 3.2 needs replacement. IV KCl per protocol (never IV push, always on pump, never >10 mEq/hr peripheral).
- (E) Correct — The combination of hypotension, tachycardia, fever, elevated WBC, and low O₂ sat requires provider notification.
- (F) Correct — If sepsis is suspected, blood cultures should be drawn BEFORE antibiotics are started.
- (G) Incorrect — D5W is not appropriate for glucose management. The patient has hyperglycemia; D5W would worsen it. Sliding scale insulin is appropriate.
- (H) Correct — Incentive spirometry prevents atelectasis (the most common cause of early post-op fever and low O₂ sat).
- (I) Correct — Review PCA history to determine if overuse is contributing to sedation. This is assessment before intervention.
- (J) Incorrect — Ambulation is important but NOT appropriate while the patient is hypotensive and hemodynamically unstable. Stabilize first.

Question 5 — Take Action (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

Instructions: Complete the following statements by selecting from the drop-down options.

Statement 1: The nurse should first address the patient's [hypotension / hypokalemia / fever / hyperglycemia] because [it is the most life-threatening finding / it will improve the patient's pain / the provider ordered it first / it is causing the elevated WBC].

Answer: hypotension | it is the most life-threatening finding

Statement 2: Before notifying the provider, the nurse should [administer IV potassium / obtain a full set of current vital signs and assess the surgical site / increase the PCA dose / transfer the patient to the ICU] to provide a comprehensive report.

Answer: obtain a full set of current vital signs and assess the surgical site

Statement 3: The nurse should [hold / continue / increase / discontinue] the morphine PCA and apply [a non-rebreather mask at 15 L/min / a nasal cannula at 2 L/min / a Venturi mask at 40% / no supplemental oxygen] based on the current assessment.

Answer: hold | a nasal cannula at 2 L/min

Rationale:
- Hypotension takes priority because cardiovascular compromise threatens all organ systems. Using ABCs: adequate circulation is required before other issues can be effectively addressed.
- Before calling the provider, gather a complete picture — recheck vitals, assess the surgical site for bleeding or signs of infection, check JP drain output, review I&O, and assess urine output. This allows an SBAR report.
- Holding the PCA (not discontinuing — that's a provider order) is appropriate because the patient shows signs of oversedation. A nasal cannula at 2 L/min is appropriate for an O₂ sat of 93% — a non-rebreather at 15 L/min is for severe hypoxemia (O₂ sat <85% or acute respiratory distress).

SCENARIO 2: Acute Heart Failure Exacerbation (Cardiac)

Clinical Setting

Cardiac step-down unit, night shift.

Patient Information

Mrs. Dorothy Andersen, 72 years old. Admitted for acute exacerbation of heart failure (HFrEF, EF 25%). History of atrial fibrillation, type 2 diabetes, and chronic kidney disease stage 3. Home medications: lisinopril 20 mg daily, carvedilol 12.5 mg BID, furosemide 40 mg PO daily, apixaban 5 mg BID, metformin 500 mg BID, and potassium chloride 20 mEq daily. Admitted weight: 176 lbs (80 kg). Dry weight per chart: 162 lbs (73.6 kg).

Nurse's Notes — 2200

Patient sitting upright in bed, appears anxious and short of breath. States "I can't catch my breath when I lie flat." Using 2 pillows at home for the past week but tonight needs to sit straight up. Reports 3-day history of worsening ankle swelling and "my shoes won't fit." Has been eating canned soups and deli meats because she's been "too tired to cook." Bilateral crackles in lower lung fields. 3+ pitting edema bilateral lower extremities to mid-shin. Jugular venous distension noted at 45 degrees. Abdomen distended, patient reports feeling "bloated." Daily weight today: 182 lbs (82.7 kg) — a gain of 6 lbs (2.7 kg) since admission yesterday.

Vital Signs — 2200

Parameter Value
Temperature 98.4°F (36.9°C)
Heart Rate 112 bpm (irregular)
Blood Pressure 158/94 mmHg
Respiratory Rate 28/min
O₂ Saturation 88% on room air

Lab Results

Test Result Reference
BNP 1,840 pg/mL <100 (normal), >400 (HF likely)
Potassium 5.4 mEq/L 3.5–5.0
Sodium 131 mEq/L 135–145
BUN 42 mg/dL 5–20
Creatinine 2.1 mg/dL 0.6–1.3
Digoxin level 2.4 ng/mL 0.8–2.0
Glucose 156 mg/dL 70–110

Question 1 — Recognize Cues (Highlight Format)

Instructions: From the data above, select ALL findings that indicate worsening heart failure or complications requiring immediate nursing attention.

  • [ ] A. Patient sitting upright, anxious, short of breath
  • [ ] B. Orthopnea (needs to sit straight up)
  • [ ] C. 3-day history of worsening ankle swelling
  • [ ] D. Has been eating canned soups and deli meats
  • [ ] E. Bilateral crackles in lower lung fields
  • [ ] F. 3+ pitting edema bilateral lower extremities
  • [ ] G. JVD at 45 degrees
  • [ ] H. Weight gain of 6 lbs since yesterday
  • [ ] I. Heart rate 112, irregular
  • [ ] J. Blood pressure 158/94
  • [ ] K. Respiratory rate 28
  • [ ] L. O₂ saturation 88%
  • [ ] M. BNP 1,840 pg/mL
  • [ ] N. Potassium 5.4 mEq/L
  • [ ] O. Sodium 131 mEq/L
  • [ ] P. Digoxin level 2.4 ng/mL
  • [ ] Q. BUN 42, Creatinine 2.1

Correct Answers: All — A through Q

Rationale: Every single finding in this scenario is clinically significant. This is a patient in acute decompensated heart failure with multiple complications:

  • A, B, E, K, L — Left-sided heart failure signs: dyspnea, orthopnea, crackles (fluid in lungs), tachypnea, hypoxemia. Pulmonary congestion is worsening.
  • C, F, G, H — Right-sided heart failure signs: peripheral edema, JVD, abdominal distension, rapid weight gain (1 kg = ~1 L fluid; she's gained 2.7 kg = ~2.7 L fluid in one day).
  • D — High sodium diet is a direct contributor to fluid retention and exacerbation.
  • I — Tachycardia + irregular rhythm = uncontrolled atrial fibrillation, which worsens cardiac output.
  • J — Hypertension increases afterload, further stressing the failing heart.
  • M — BNP 1,840 confirms severe heart failure (>400 is diagnostic threshold).
  • N — K⁺ 5.4 is above normal. With CKD, impaired renal excretion is likely. Risk for cardiac arrhythmias. Also concerning because she's on lisinopril (ACE inhibitor) and taking KCl supplement.
  • O — Na⁺ 131 = dilutional hyponatremia from fluid overload. Common in severe HF.
  • P — Digoxin 2.4 is TOXIC (therapeutic: 0.8–2.0). With impaired renal function, digoxin clearance is reduced. Hold digoxin, monitor for toxicity signs (N/V, visual halos, bradycardia — though A-fib masks the bradycardia).
  • Q — BUN 42, Creatinine 2.1 = worsening renal function (cardiorenal syndrome). CKD baseline may be lower — compare to previous results.

Question 2 — Analyze Cues (Cloze/Drop-Down Format)

Instructions: Complete the following analysis.

Statement 1: The patient's worsening symptoms are primarily caused by [fluid overload / dehydration / medication non-compliance / pneumonia] as evidenced by [crackles, edema, JVD, and rapid weight gain / low blood pressure and dry skin / empty pill bottles / productive cough and fever].

Answer: fluid overload | crackles, edema, JVD, and rapid weight gain

Statement 2: The patient's dietary intake of canned soups and deli meats contributed to her exacerbation because these foods are [high in sodium / high in potassium / low in protein / high in sugar], which causes [fluid retention / hyperkalemia / muscle wasting / hyperglycemia].

Answer: high in sodium | fluid retention

Statement 3: The digoxin level of 2.4 ng/mL is [therapeutic / subtherapeutic / toxic / not relevant], and the most likely contributing factor is [decreased renal clearance / increased hepatic metabolism / dietary interaction / missed doses].

Answer: toxic | decreased renal clearance

Rationale:
- HF exacerbation is almost always fluid overload, triggered by dietary sodium excess, medication non-adherence, or worsening cardiac function. This patient has clear volume overload signs (crackles, edema, JVD, weight gain).
- Canned soups and deli meats are extremely high in sodium. One can of soup can contain 800–1,200 mg sodium. Heart failure patients should limit sodium to <2,000 mg/day. She's likely been consuming 3,000+ mg/day.
- Digoxin is renally cleared. With creatinine 2.1 (worsening CKD), digoxin accumulates → toxicity. This is a critical drug-disease interaction to recognize.

Question 3 — Prioritize Hypotheses (Drag-and-Drop Format)

Instructions: Rank these patient problems from highest to lowest priority.

Priority Problem
1 Impaired gas exchange (O₂ sat 88%, crackles, dyspnea)
2 Digoxin toxicity (level 2.4, risk for fatal arrhythmia)
3 Excess fluid volume (6 lb weight gain, edema, JVD)
4 Hyperkalemia (K⁺ 5.4 with digoxin toxicity compounds risk)
5 Knowledge deficit regarding sodium-restricted diet

Rationale:
- Oxygenation is always first — ABCs. O₂ sat 88% requires immediate intervention (supplemental O₂, positioning).
- Digoxin toxicity is second because toxic digoxin levels in a patient with A-fib and hyperkalemia create serious arrhythmia risk. Hypokalemia increases dig toxicity, but hyperkalemia with dig toxicity also increases cardiac risk. Hold digoxin immediately.
- Fluid overload is third because it's the root cause of the respiratory symptoms — IV diuretics will address both the fluid and the oxygenation over time.
- Hyperkalemia is fourth — K⁺ 5.4 is mildly elevated and concerning in the context of digoxin toxicity (potassium and digoxin compete for the same binding sites), but not at immediately life-threatening levels. Hold KCl supplement, reassess after diuresis.
- Teaching is fifth — essential for preventing readmission, but not the acute priority.

Question 4 — Take Action (SATA Format)

Instructions: Select all appropriate immediate nursing actions.

  • [ ] A. Elevate the head of bed to high Fowler's position
  • [ ] B. Apply supplemental oxygen to maintain O₂ sat >95%
  • [ ] C. Hold the digoxin and notify the provider of the toxic level
  • [ ] D. Administer IV furosemide as ordered
  • [ ] E. Hold the potassium chloride supplement
  • [ ] F. Restrict oral fluids to 1,500 mL/day
  • [ ] G. Administer a normal saline bolus of 500 mL
  • [ ] H. Place patient on continuous cardiac monitoring
  • [ ] I. Weigh patient daily at the same time
  • [ ] J. Begin a low-sodium diet education session now
  • [ ] K. Insert a Foley catheter to accurately monitor urine output
  • [ ] L. Prepare to administer Digibind if provider orders it

Correct Answers: A, B, C, D, E, F, H, K, L

Rationale:
- (A) Correct — High Fowler's reduces preload, improves lung expansion, decreases work of breathing.
- (B) Correct — O₂ sat 88% needs supplemental O₂. Start with NC at 2–4 L/min, titrate up.
- (C) Correct — Digoxin level 2.4 is toxic. HOLD the drug and notify the provider immediately.
- (D) Correct — IV furosemide (not PO — IV is faster and more reliable in acute decompensation) to diurese excess fluid.
- (E) Correct — K⁺ is already 5.4 and patient is on KCl supplement. Hold it.
- (F) Correct — Fluid restriction is a core HF management strategy during acute exacerbation.
- (G) Incorrect — NS bolus would WORSEN fluid overload. This patient needs fluid removal, not addition.
- (H) Correct — With digoxin toxicity + hyperkalemia + A-fib, continuous telemetry is essential.
- (I) — Important but already being done. Not an immediate priority action at 2200. (Not wrong, but not an "immediate" action for this question.)
- (J) Incorrect — Patient is in acute respiratory distress. Teaching is important but should wait until she's stable and can focus.
- (K) Correct — Accurate I&O monitoring is critical during aggressive diuresis. A Foley allows precise measurement.
- (L) Correct — Digibind (digoxin immune fab) may be needed if the provider determines the toxicity warrants it. Having it prepared is appropriate.

Question 5 — Evaluate Outcomes (Matrix Format)

Instructions: Four hours later, after IV furosemide 80 mg, supplemental O₂ at 4L NC, and holding digoxin, the nurse reassesses. Evaluate each finding.

Finding at 0200 Expected/Improving Concerning/Unexpected
O₂ sat 94% on 4L NC X
Urine output: 800 mL in 4 hours X
BP 138/82 X
HR 88, irregular X
Patient reports seeing yellow-green halos around lights X
RR 22 X
Crackles now in bases only (were in lower 1/3 bilaterally) X
Patient able to recline to 45 degrees without dyspnea X

Rationale:
- Most findings show improvement: oxygenation improving, excellent urine output (diuresis working), BP decreasing toward target, HR improved, crackles resolving, tolerating lower position.
- Visual halos are a classic sign of digoxin toxicity and indicate the toxicity is producing symptoms despite holding the drug. Digoxin has a long half-life (36 hours, longer with renal impairment). This finding should be reported to the provider — Digibind may now be indicated.
